| OBL-C25 | **SATISFIED** | The cumulative-supply overlay is re-derived by the host rather than passively mirrored | `M10` (sync-audit) | H | **SATISFIED — the host recomputes rather than trusting the WASM overlay, and says so at the site. Read 2026-09-22.** `read_cumulative_from_overlay` (`bin/dwowd/src/block_acceptor.rs:588`) carries the `M10` comment and does the work: *"do NOT trust the WASM overlay mirror blindly. Recompute `S_H = S_{H-1} + C_base` from the coinbase's value commitment and the previous supply entry, then cross-check against what WASM wrote. A divergence means the WASM overlay and the host disagree on supply accounting — reject the block"* (`:656-660`). The re-derivation reads the **previous** entry from `chain_state.supply_chain` (`:666-668`) and compares the recomputed value-commit, blind and total supply against the overlay's entry, rejecting on any of the three. So the overlay is a mirror the host checks, not a value it stores on trust — which is what the row asks for. **One precision, checked rather than assumed**: the check is deliberately conditional — it fires only when the coinbase's first call is `PoWRewardV1` whose params decode, and skips silently otherwise (`:661-664`, where a failed decode becomes `None` rather than an error). That is safe because a block whose coinbase params do not decode cannot reach here at all: `validate_block_structure` hard-errors on exactly that decode (`src/linear/src/validation.rs:432-433`), and it runs earlier in `accept_block`. Worth stating because the skip is invisible in this function and a future reordering of those two steps would turn it into a hole. **Gap: no negative control** — nothing constructs an overlay entry that disagrees with the recomputation and asserts the rejection, so the cross-check is enforced but unpinned |
| OBL-C26 | **CLOSED** | `uncle_commitment_set` is reconstructible after a restart — persisted, or derived on demand. A restart cannot currently reconstruct it | genesis-consensus open item 3 | M | **OPEN — the premise is true, and the hazard is moot: the set is write-only. Read 2026-09-22.** Both halves verified. **(i) It is created empty on restart.** `chain_state.rs:379-382` constructs `Mutex::new(HashMap::new())` under a `TODO(Phase 2)`, and says why the old restoration was deleted rather than repaired: it read `store.uncles` — which holds JSON-serialized `UncleBlock` values, not `u64` heights — so its `v.len() == 8` filter discarded every entry and "this was always empty". So a restart really cannot reconstruct it. The recomputation it says is possible is indeed possible and deterministic: `r_i = blake3(uncle_hash ‖ u_i ‖ H)` then `C = u_i·G_v + r_i·G_r` is how each entry is built (`:1086-1114`), and `UncleBlock` (`block.rs:184-200`) persists the header, the transactions, `pin_accepted` and `pin_confirmed` — everything the formula needs — while carrying **no** commitment field of its own. Nothing derives it. **(ii) Nothing reads it, so the gap decides nothing.** The only uses of `uncle_commitment_set` in the tree are the insert after a successful sled commit (`:1479-1481`) and the retain-on-reverse in `disconnect_block` (`:1931-1932`); there is no `get`, no `contains_key`, no iteration, no public accessor reading it, and no other file in `src/` or `bin/` names it. `has_commitment()` (`:654-656`) reads `commitment_set`, a different structure. Uncle dedup — the need this set looks like it was built for — is met elsewhere and sled-backed, by `stored_uncle_hashes()` (`:743`, consumed as `existing_keys` by `check_uncles`). **So the row should not be read as a restart-divergence hazard: it is dead state** — memory and a mutex acquisition per block for a map no decision consults — and the fix is a choice between deleting it and implementing the read that would justify it, not a persistence patch. **One stale comment found while checking this**, recorded because it is the kind of thing that makes a reader believe the opposite: `:1084-1085` says the entries are "computed before the closure so uncle commitment batch is included in the atomic sled transaction" — they are not; the only sled batches for that height take blocks, uncles and *claim* commitments (`:1216-1286`), and `uncle_commitment_entries` has exactly one consumer, the in-memory insert. **CLOSED 2026-09-23 by deletion, not by persistence** — the row's first option, taken deliberately. Gone: the field, its construction, the per-uncle Pedersen computation that fed it (a blake3 and a Pedersen commitment per accepted uncle, per block, whose only consumer was that insert), the post-commit insert, and the disconnect-path reversal. The stale comment above went with them. The claim that it is *dead* was re-checked across the whole tree before deleting, not inherited: `uncle_commitment_set` and `uncle_commitment_entries` appeared in **no** file outside `chain_state.rs`, and inside it the set was written, rolled back, and never read — no `contains_key`, no iteration, no accessor; the "phantom already-included markers" the disconnect path guarded against were visible only to a reader of a set that had none. Deleting is also the safe direction: the values are recomputable from chain data, so if the read that would justify them is ever built, nothing has been lost. **What the deletion exposed is now its own row, `OBL-C85`**: the per-uncle Pedersen audit commitment the spec says lives in the stored cumulative supply chain is not stored anywhere — this cache was the only place the points were ever materialised, and it was not durable either |
| OBL-C27 | **CLOSED** | `stored_uncle_hashes` compares keys without zero-padding ambiguity: correct only while every key is a 32-byte blake3 hash, which is a fragile invariant rather than an enforced one | genesis-consensus open item 5 | M | **OPEN (latent) — the invariant is real, unenforced, and currently unviolable. Read 2026-09-22.** The function (`chain_state.rs:743-754`) maps each sled key to a fixed `[u8; 32]` by copying `min(32, key.len())` bytes into a zeroed array, so a 30-byte key `K` and the 32-byte key `K‖00 00` become the **same** array — the zero-padding ambiguity the row describes, and it is silent: the copy is an iterator `zip`, not a length check. What bounds the exposure today is that `store.uncles` has exactly one insertion site, `uncles_batch.insert(uncle_hash.as_bytes(), …)` (`:1171`), where the argument is a `blake3::Hash` and therefore always 32 bytes — a *typed* guarantee at one call site rather than an enforced property of the reader. So the row's "fragile invariant rather than an enforced one" is exactly right, and it is worth keeping open rather than closing on the strength of the current caller. **The fix is small but is a semantic change, not a decoration**: reject or refuse a non-32-byte key instead of padding it, so the reader fails closed on a key shape it cannot compare unambiguously. Not done here — a drive-by change to a consensus-state accessor, for a case that cannot currently occur, is the kind of edit this register exists to prevent unless it is batched with a reason. **CLOSED 2026-09-23 by the batched change, with the reason.** `stored_uncle_hashes` now returns `Result<HashSet<[u8; 32]>, LinearError>` and a key that is not exactly 32 bytes is a `StorageError` naming its length; an **iteration** error is also an error now, where `if let Ok(..)` previously skipped a failed read and returned a set that looked complete. The signature change was the honest form of "refuse" here: the only caller is `block_acceptor::accept_block`, which already maps `block_target_at`'s error into a block rejection, so failing closed means the block is refused rather than deduped against a padded guess — the alternative to a hard error was a *silently different* dedup set, which is the one outcome a dedup input must not have. **Control**: `test_uncle_hash_reader_refuses_a_non_32_byte_key` stores a 32-byte key and asserts it reads back (so the reader is not simply broken), then stores a 30-byte key and asserts `Err(StorageError)` — the exact input the old reader padded into `K‖00 00`. **Negative-controlled**: restoring the padding makes it fail, recorded. Pin-neutral: `src/linear/src/chain_state.rs` is off the hashing and serialization path. **And the rule's model abstracts this row rather than re-deriving it, 2026-09-24.** `Consensus/UncleRules.lean` uses *distinctness of headers* as its stand-in for the dedup key and says so in its module note, in this row's own terms — the key form is an encoding the model does not have. Where the abstraction is load-bearing the model is explicit about which two uncles do *not* collide: the two uncles in `three_uncles_at_one_depth_satisfy_every_rule` carry different declared miners, so their headers differ and the dedup key would not refuse the second or third — which is the fact that makes the set admissible and its pins over-large at the same time. So this row's "fragile invariant rather than an enforced one" is bordered by a model that states what it assumes instead of assuming it silently. |
| OBL-C28 | **CLOSED** | `competing_seen` cannot grow unbounded from network input | genesis-consensus open item 6 | M | **Audited 2026-09-23: PARTLY, not CLOSED** — the `competing_blocks` bound holds, but `competing_seen` does not: both insert paths add the dedup key *before* the per-height cap test (`chain_state.rs:955` then `:964`), and take/prune remove keys only for blocks they actually hold, so an over-cap key has no removal path and the set grows from network input. Originally closed 2026-09-22, and it was two rows deep.** The premise was stale: `prune_competing` is *not* "called only when blocks are taken" — `connect_block` calls it on every block (`:1486`, behind the `pow` feature gate), and it drops every height below `MAX_UNCLE_DEPTH`, so the bound is `MAX_UNCLE_DEPTH × MAX_COMPETING_BLOCKS` and network input cannot exceed it. (A `never used` warning for this method appears in configurations *without* `pow`, where `connect_block` does not exist — that is what made the row look open, and it is a warning about a different build.) **But the bound was defeated in effect**: `prune_competing`'s removals keyed on the serialization hash while the inserts keyed on `hash_with_vm`, so before `OBL-C52` was fixed every prune removed nothing and the set really did grow unboundedly — twice over, since `take_competing_blocks`'s removals were also no-ops. So this row closes as a consequence of `OBL-C52`'s fix rather than by its own change: the structure was always right and the keys were not. Recorded because a reader who finds "C28 closed" and then sees the remove-key fix under C52 would otherwise have to re-derive the connection. **The leak the 2026-09-23 audit found is now closed too, and it is a real one that survives any fix to the keys**: `competing_seen` is cleaned only for blocks that are actually stored — `take_competing_blocks` and `prune_competing` remove a key while handing back or dropping the block that owns it — so a key inserted for a block the per-height cap then *refused* has no removal path at all and lives until restart, while the set is fed by whoever relays blocks. **Three insert sites, not the one the audit named**: the competing-at-tip branch, the uncle-chain-extension branch, and `store_competing_block` (which `accept_block` reaches with a block a peer chose). Each now tests the cap **before** recording the key. `store_competing_block` was written *not* to nest locks to do this — cap in one critical section, insert and push in others — because the file's documented lock order named `competing_seen` before `competing_blocks` while `prune_competing`, the only place that holds both, has always taken `competing_blocks` first; that doc is corrected in place, and the reason the insert paths avoid nesting is spelled out there. **Control**: `test_competing_cap_refusal_leaves_no_dedup_key` drives the real `store_competing_block` to the cap, adds one more distinct block, and asserts both that the table holds at `MAX_COMPETING_BLOCKS` and that the set gained **no** key — then takes the stored blocks and stores the refused one, which must succeed, proving the refusal did not record its identity on the way out. **Negative-controlled**: with the old order restored it fails `left: 21, right: 20`, recorded verbatim — the refused block's key, present |
| OBL-C29 | **CLOSED** | The genesis filter modes (`Off`/`Relaxed`/`Strict`) and the Path-B tie-breaker are implemented in Rust, or the Python model is amended — they are specified and absent | genesis-consensus open item 1 | M | **PARTLY — and the two halves are in different places, which is what the row could not say.** *In the model the modes are real*: `apply_genesis_filter(our_genesis, peer_tips, mode)` with `mode: "Off" \| "Relaxed" \| "Strict"` (`contrib/model/chain_validation_model.py:3667-3720`) — Off accepts all peers without filtering, Relaxed falls back to all peers if the filter empties, Strict keeps an empty result empty — and each is exercised (`:3737`, `:3754`, `:3766`, `:3775`). *In the machine they do not exist*: the vocabulary appears nowhere in `src/`, `bin/` or `contrib/` outside that file, so there is no mode parameter, and the Rust implements **Path A only** — `sync_connection.rs:442-455`, where a peer presenting a **different** genesis hash is incompatible and a peer presenting **none** is admitted because it is a bootstrapping joiner whose genesis is validated against the pin downstream. That admission is the *hard-coded* equivalent of one mode, not a parameterised one. *Path B is deferred with its reason in the code*, exactly as the "premises that moved" note says: *"Path B (the plurality vote over peer tips at height 0) is a multi-peer decision and cannot be made on a single connection, so a node with no genesis still accepts and lets the filter run once tips are known"* (`:448-451`). So the row's disjunction is unmet on both arms — not implemented in Rust, not removed from the model — and the honest status is the split: Path A live, Path B deferred-with-reason, the mode parameterisation model-only. **PARTLY, and the model side chosen — 2026-09-23.** Since the Python model *is* the specification (`python-model-is-the-spec`), the resolution is a port rather than an amendment: `sync_boundary::apply_genesis_filter` is the model's function with its names (`Off`/`Relaxed`/`Strict`), its two paths, and **its tie-breaker**, which is load-bearing rather than defensive — on equal votes `Some(hash)` must beat `None`, or the only peer holding a real genesis is the one filtered out and a height-0 node syncs from nobody. A `BTreeMap` and an explicit `(count, is_some)` key replace the model's `Counter` + sort for the same reason the model itself replaced its map: the answer must not depend on iteration order. **Where it is, and why it could not go where the row pointed**: the model's docstring says it "matches `consensus_linear.rs:181-258`", but the Rust implementation sits in the per-connection *handshake* — where the peer *set* is not visible, which is precisely why Path B was deferred there with a reason that still holds ("a plurality vote is a multi-peer decision and cannot be made on a single connection"). The peer set is visible in the sync loop, and `PeerTip` has carried `genesis_hash` all along while nothing read it: the boundary type was built for this filter. **The wiring into the sync loop is the one piece not landed**, and its absence is the only reason this row is not closed: it is a four-line change in `bin/dwowd/src/task/consensus_linear.rs`, and that file currently carries *another session's uncommitted negative control* — `admit_received_genesis` with its pin check replaced by `Ok(())` under a comment reading "NEGATIVE CONTROL — REMOVE". Committing an edit to that file now would carry a **disabled consensus check** into the commit, so the wiring waits for the file to be quiet. Until then the filter is dead code in the strict sense — tested, unused — which is recorded here rather than left to be discovered. **Control**: four ported tests, including the model's own tie-breaker case and its `Off` control (so a "keeps everything" function cannot pass); **negative-controlled** by inverting the tie-break key, which fails with `left: [1] right: [0]` — the genesis-less peer kept instead of the real one, recorded verbatim. `cargo test -p dwow_chain --lib --all-features` → 278 passed, 0 failed. **And the wiring landed the same day, so the row closes: the sync loop now applies the filter to the peer set before it decides which peers to pull from, `Strict`.** The four parts are one edit: tips are collected *with the peer that answered each*, the filter runs over those tips, `max_peer_height` is taken over the compatible ones only (a height this node cannot validate is not a height worth chasing), and the peers the filter rejects are dropped from the pass — which closes their connections. Two details are chosen rather than inherited: a peer that is merely **silent** (never answered a tip request) keeps its place, because a timeout is the spec's "simply skipped" case and not something this rule may exclude — what is dropped is exactly the peers whose tip the filter refused; and the mode is a named `const GENESIS_FILTER: GenesisFilterMode = Strict`, so the choice is visible at the call site rather than implied by the function's shape. **Verification, and it is now end-to-end**: `cargo test --release -p dwowd --all-features --lib daemon_sync_integration` → **10 passed, 0 failed**, including `test_daemon_pull_sync_converges`, which drives two daemons syncing over the rail — the loop this filter now sits in. A filter that wrongly rejected a compatible peer fails that test, so the integration control the row lacked exists after the wiring, in the form of the test that already covered the loop. *(The earlier version of this note said the wiring was held back because `consensus_linear.rs` carried another session's disabled genesis-pin negative control; that control was restored the same hour and the wiring proceeded, so the note is corrected rather than left standing.)* |
| OBL-C30 | **SATISFIED** | `compute_reward`/`verify_uncle_split` signatures agree between the model and the Rust; the connect path's one-element slice is equivalent but reads oddly | genesis-consensus open item 2 | **SATISFIED — the signatures agree parameter for parameter, and the odd-looking slice is equivalent with its reason written at the site. Read 2026-09-22.** Rust: `verify_uncle_split(base_reward: BlockReward, canonical_reward: BlockReward, uncle_pin_confirmed: &[BlockReward])` (`src/linear/src/supply_chain.rs:313-317`). Model: `verify_uncle_split(base_reward: int, canonical_reward: int, uncle_pin_confirmed: List[int])` (`contrib/model/chain_model.py:217-220`) — same arity, same names, same order, and the docstring states the invariant in the same words (*"canonical_reward + sum(uncle_pin_confirmed) == base_reward"*) while naming the Rust function it mirrors; `compute_reward` likewise (`chain_model.py:200`). The body is the same two lines: sum the pins, then require the exact equality (`supply_chain.rs:318-319`; `chain_model.py:226-231`). **The one-element slice is equivalent and the code says why**: the connect path calls it with `&[total_pin]` where `total_pin = crate::block::total_accepted_pin(uncles)?` (`chain_state.rs:1071-1079`) — the *sum* wrapped in a slice — and since the callee sums its argument, a one-element slice of the sum and the per-uncle list produce the same total; the comment above the call records exactly that ("Same Σ-pin semantics as the host check and the builder (`total_accepted_pin`)"). The multi-element shape is not left untested either: `supply_chain.rs:437-450` covers single-pin and multi-pin calls against the same equality | M |
| OBL-C31 | **SATISFIED** | The generic-prover write path enforces the derived-rule DAG at parse time — a forward reference or a cycle is a parse error, not an unhandled case | l1-write-path `V1`–`V4` (the DAG extension); `V5`–`V7` already applied | M | **Audited 2026-09-23: SATISFIED, not PARTLY** — the derived-rule DAG *is* enforced at parse time, in `src/sdk/src/prover.rs:201-217`, pinned by `witness_map_rejects_derived_forward_reference` and its positive control `witness_map_allows_input_forward_reference`; added `4a6e5c5609` (2026-08-27), before this row was written. Read 2026-09-22.** The generic prover is `bin/dww/src/prover_impl.rs`, and it evaluates `DerivedRule`s in order against a slot vector. A forward reference is caught by `operand()` (`:485-496`), which reads an *already-bound* slot and otherwise returns a clear error — `"witness[{idx}]: derived operand slot {i} is unbound or out of range"` — so the case is handled rather than unhandled, exactly as the register's "premises that moved" note says. Two things the row's requirement does not get. **First, it is not a parse-time check, because there is no parse stage**: a search of the file for `parse`, `DAG`, `cycle` or `topolog` finds nothing — the rules are evaluated as they are read, and the only safety net is that the slot must already be bound. **Second, a cycle cannot be expressed in this representation**: a rule referencing a later slot *is* a forward reference, so cycles collapse into the case above rather than needing separate enforcement. So the obligation is satisfied in substance (no unhandled case, and no expressible cycle) and unsatisfied in form (no DAG validated at parse time). **Gap: the error path is unpinned** — the message occurs only at its definition, and none of the file's three tests exercises it |

The disposition — accepted with reason, the quarantine not lifted — is at the end of this cell.** `BARBS` is real: declared on the `Message` trait with an empty default (`src/net/message.rs:71`, `:109`) and overridable by the macros (`:85`, `:140`), and the sync layer sets it (`SYNC_BARBS`, `sync_types.rs:322`) and passes it into the boundary codec (`:357` → `dwow_core::impl_boundary_codec!`) for all four messages. **Enforcement, however, does not run**: the accessor that would publish a message's barbs to the channel-boundary rule is commented out — `message_publisher.rs:318` (`// fn barbs(&self) -> &'static [crate::barb::BarbId] { M::BARBS }`) — and the nearby note says when it returns: *"reinstated when quarantine boundary enforcement is activated (§10.4)"* (`:247`, `:319`). So this is a **third** state, not one of the row's two: deferred-by-design with the reason recorded in code and pointing at `type-system.md` §10.4 ("Bridging — Shared Channels with Typed Barbs"). Recording it as such, because "DEFERRED" in the carried-from column and a live constant in the tree is exactly the pair a reader would otherwise read as enforcement. **Closed as ACCEPTED-WITH-REASON 2026-09-23**, which is the row's second arm taken deliberately: the quarantine is *not* lifted, so the accessor is not reinstated, and the deferral is recorded where a reader meets it — the register here, and the code at `message_publisher.rs:247`/`:319` with its §10.4 pointer. The residual is the row's first arm, unchanged: reinstating `barbs()` is what lifting the quarantine requires, and until then `BARBS` is a declaration the net layer does not consult |
| OBL-C33 | **CLOSED** | A per-peer failure score persists across sync passes, so a peer that fails is deprioritised rather than reset each tick | node-sync `F5`, PARTIALLY RESOLVED (`sync-protocol.md` §13.3) | M | **OPEN — searched for, not sampled: no per-peer score exists anywhere in the tree, while the spec requires one by name. Read 2026-09-22.** A repo-wide search of `src/`, `bin/` and `crates/` for `misbehav`, `ban_peer`, `peer_score`, `banned` and `disconnect_peer` returns nothing that scores or penalises a peer — the only hits are unrelated (a Tor-connection comment in `net/channel.rs:651`, a hostname fixture, a propagation-rate comment). The spec is explicit and the row quotes it correctly: *"Peer discipline SHALL be a single persistent score (Bitcoin Core `Misbehaving()`): a peer that serves an **invalid block** is disconnected; a peer that times out is simply skipped and the next peer tried"* (`doc/src/arch/sync-protocol.md:355-356`). What the code implements is the **second** clause only — the pull loop skips a failing peer within a pass and tries the next (§13.3 step 4, `bin/dwowd/src/task/consensus_linear.rs`) — and it persists nothing across the 30-second ticks, so a peer that lied in pass N is treated exactly like a fresh peer in pass N+1. Nor is there a disconnection path for an invalid-block server: the only `disconnect` in the sync task refers to *blocks* in a reorg (`:285`, `:293`), not peers. Worth noting the adjacent rule the code does record, so it is not mistaken for this one: `chain_state.rs:865` — *"§14.3 — a duplicate SHALL be skipped, never banned"* — is about duplicate *blocks*. **CLOSED 2026-09-23, and the plumbing was already there — with a comment saying so.** `SyncPeer` has carried a `url` field since it was written, documented verbatim as *"stable identity for per-peer scoring/punishment"*, and nothing ever read it: the identity for exactly this rule was built and the rule was not. It now has an accessor, and `LinearSyncClient` holds a `PeerScores` — one counter per dialed URL, kept for the life of the node, so it survives the pass that observed the misbehaviour (a `SyncPeer` is a fresh object every dial, and `sync_peers` is a per-pass local, which is why the score cannot live on the peer). **Both clauses of the spec are implemented as the spec draws them, not as the row phrases it**: a peer that serves an invalid block is scored and, at `MISBEHAVIOUR_LIMIT = 1`, is not dialed again — while a timeout scores nothing at all, because the spec's second clause says a timed-out peer is "simply skipped". The row's word "deprioritised" is therefore *narrowed* here rather than implemented: there is no priority ordering, no decay, no backoff and no slower-failing-peer bucket, all of which the spec's own next sentence rejects as machinery with no production analogue. **Which blocks are the peer's fault is the judgement the fix had to make, and it is split deliberately**: the contiguity guard, the genesis magic-byte check, the proof-of-token-balance failure, the pre-genesis-height block and the genesis-pin mismatch all score; a RandomX cache/VM allocation failure and a genesis *hashing* failure do not (both are local — the code already called them "local failure, retrying"), and neither does an `accept_block` failure, which may be a legitimate fork between two honest nodes and is the one case the reorg path exists for. The spec's protection is now reachable: `dial_sync_peers` checks the ban per dial, so a liar is excluded on the very next tick rather than re-observed. **Control**: `test_a_misbehaving_peer_is_banned_and_others_are_not` scores one peer and asserts it is banned at the limit, that the score accumulates rather than resetting, and — the control — that an unrelated peer is untouched, so the score is keyed rather than global. The ban is **not** asserted end to end: there is no harness that dials a lying peer, so the row records the unit-level control and the absence of an integration one |
| OBL-C34 | **CLOSED** | The fail-closed genesis handshake and its bootstrap path are re-read against the `P2-7` rework before this finding is called closed: the state machine folded `WaitingForGenesis` into `Behind` (`bin/dwowd/src/lib.rs:161-179`) and the peer's genesis is no longer compared in the sync handshake (`linear_genesis_hash` survives only on the merge-mining path), so the analysis describes a handshake that has since changed | daemon-sync `V1`–`V4` | H | **Audited 2026-09-23: REFUTED — this is OPEN.** The genesis pin is not enforced on the bootstrap path: `check_genesis_pin`'s only production call sites are `bin/dwowd/src/lib.rs:893` and `:910`, both inside `init_linear`'s `create_genesis == true` branch, while every miner and observer runs `CREATE_GENESIS=false`, dials with no genesis, is admitted, and has its received genesis checked only by a four-byte magic test (`bin/dwowd/src/task/consensus_linear.rs:382-388`). `apply_genesis_deployments` (`src/linear/src/execution.rs:902`) then deploys whatever WASM bytes the block carries — there is no pin comparison in that file. The comment the closure rested on (`src/linear/src/sync_connection.rs:446`) names a call that does not happen. **Originally: SATISFIED 2026-09-22 — the evidence is named in the "Verified 2026-09-22" section below.** **PARTLY 2026-09-23 — the pin now bites on the adopt path; the seam's own refusal is unmeasured, and that is the residue.** *The change.* `bin/dwowd/src/task/consensus_linear.rs`, in the pull loop every miner and observer takes: when the pulled block is the genesis its hash is computed and passed to `check_genesis_pin(_, "received")` **before** `accept_block` sees it, and a mismatch drops that peer rather than aborting sync. Three placement facts, each read rather than chosen. It needs the block hash, which is RandomX over the header, so it sits after the cache is allocated rather than beside the four magic bytes it complements. It is deliberately **not** in `accept_block`, which is also the tests' fixture path and is called by five entry points including the miner's own production — and `check_genesis_pin`'s own doc says the pin "has to bite on the path that creates *and adopts* a chain" while warning that it "must not sit inside the shared fixture, where its failure is indistinguishable from the test's own failure". And it is the genesis only, so the cost is one hash per sync. *What is measured.* `test_daemon_pull_sync_converges` — the fresh-node pull from height 0, so the genesis does come through this loop — passes with the check in place: **5m10s wall / 46 core-minutes**, recorded because the genesis bootstrap re-executes all nine deployments, and the log carries **zero** occurrences of the refusal. So the check does not obstruct a correct genesis. It does **not** follow that the check ran: a condition that was never true would look identical. *What is not measured, and the two ways to close it.* The end-to-end negative — a wrong pin making this seam refuse a correct genesis — was attempted and **blocked by the sandbox classifier**, because it requires a deliberately wrong `bin/dwowd/genesis_hash.txt` on disk. The pin was restored byte-identically and `genesis_pin_is_current` re-run green (2.22s) before anything else was done. What stands instead is a composition, and this register's history is that seams fail exactly where functions pass: `genesis_pin_rejects_a_wrong_hash` (`bin/dwowd/src/tests/genesis.rs:567`) already asserts both sides of the *function* — `Ok` for the correct hash, `Err` for a wrong one, with a distinct remedy for `"stored"` vs `"computed"` — and the seam calls it on the one iteration whose height is `GENESIS`. Either run the wrong-pin variant by hand, or add the fixture it wants (a second genesis from different keys) and drive this loop with it. Until one of those exists the row stays `PARTLY` rather than `CLOSED`.** **CLOSED 2026-09-23 — the second of those two ways was taken: the fixture was added, and the loop's own decision was driven with it.** The decision was extracted from the pull loop into `task::consensus_linear::admit_received_genesis(block, hash)`, which is the function the loop now calls, and `tests::genesis::the_adopt_path_refuses_a_second_genesis` drives it with a **second genesis built by the same `build_genesis_block` with the same `DRKW_MAGIC`, differing from the pinned genesis in exactly one input: the mining key.** No deliberately wrong pin is written to disk, so the sandbox obstruction that blocked the by-hand variant does not arise and nothing is risked on a failed run. *Three assertions, and the third is the one that keeps the others honest*: the pinned genesis is admitted; the second genesis is refused with the pin named; and a block at another height is admitted whatever its hash carries — without which a function refusing everything would pass. *Negative control, run*: with the seam returning `Ok(())` unconditionally the test fails with "a genesis that is not this build's genesis MUST be refused on the adopt path", and with the real body restored it passes in 2.00s. `cargo test --release -p dwowd --all-features the_adopt_path_refuses_a_second_genesis -- --test-threads 4`. *One thing the fixture taught, recorded because the next person will hit it*: `SecretKey::from_bytes` is `pallas::Base::from_repr`, which reads the 32 bytes **little-endian**, so an arbitrary constant like `…feedface` is not a canonical field element — the first version of the fixture was rejected with "keys.toml: invalid secret key" and is now the canonical key with its last byte changed. *What this closes and what it does not*: what was unmeasured — that the loop's genesis admission refuses a foreign genesis — is now measured on the loop's own function; `test_daemon_pull_sync_converges` (5m10s) still carries the positive half end to end. Attribution: the extraction was staged by the concurrent session's commit `9a48dca6f7`; the test is this session's **And the positive half was re-measured on the current tree rather than inherited.** `test_daemon_pull_sync_converges` — the fresh-node pull from height 0, so the genesis does come through the loop and through this seam — is **1 passed, 151.80s**, and the log carries **zero** occurrences of `"does not match the compiled-in pin"`. So the seam does not obstruct a correct genesis *and* does not fire spuriously on one: the two halves now sit in one place, the fixture-driven negative above and this end-to-end positive. (The row previously recorded 5m10s for this test; the current measurement is 151.80s. The time is machine state, not a change in the test — noted so a later reader does not read the difference as one.) |

### The consensus HAZID's residue (`OBL-C35`+)

The consensus HAZID report was a *hazard register*, not a findings list: 4 CATASTROPHIC, 12 HIGH and
18 MEDIUM, each named and each with a status. Its six root causes are `RC1`–`RC12` material now, and
three hazards it records as RESOLVED stay resolved — `H-C1` (fork selection reconnects through the
full pipeline), `H-C3` (the exponential reward schedule, `fixed_pow_decay`), `H-H1` (consensus
atomics are `SeqCst` or `Acquire`/`Release`); `H-H5` (`serde_json` block storage) is closed above.
Everything else it left open is below, one proposition per hazard, because collapsing 28 named
hazards into a summary is how a HAZOP ends up with items nobody can find.

| ID | Status | Proposition | HAZID | Sev |
|---|---|---|---|
| OBL-C35 | **CLOSED** | A stratum-mined block is broadcast on accept, as the merge-mining path now does — the stratum path commits locally and peers learn of it only on the 30-second sync poll | `H-C2`, CATASTROPHIC, residual | M | **Audited 2026-09-23: CLOSED, not OPEN** — `stratum_submit`'s `CanonicalExtension` arm now calls `broadcast_block` with the block's own uncles (`bin/dwowd/src/rpc/stratum.rs:711`), matching the merge-mining path it was told to match. Originally confirmed open, and now scoped exactly: stratum is the *only* accepting path in the tree that does not broadcast. Verified 2026-09-22.** Enumerating every `broadcast_block` call site in `bin/dwowd/src/` leaves one gap: the built-in miner broadcasts (`lib.rs:1861`), the RPC miner broadcasts **with its uncles** (`rpc/miner.rs:302`), merge mining broadcasts (`rpc/mm_rpc.rs:749`, with `vec![]`), the receive path relays onward (`lib.rs:1043`), and the tests call it directly — while `stratum_submit`'s `CanonicalExtension` arm (`rpc/stratum.rs:681-701`) marks the mined transactions from the mempool and pushes the next job, and **never broadcasts**. So a stratum-mined block reaches peers only when their 30-second poll happens to fetch it, which is the catastrophic-residual shape the row names. **The fix is a port, not a design**: `mm_rpc.rs:745-747` already carries the HAZID H-C2 comment and the call, and `stratum.rs`'s handler is `impl RequestHandler<StratumRpcHandler> for DwowNode`, so `self.p2p_handler.p2p` is in scope exactly as it is there. One refinement to take from the *other* correct site rather than from `mm_rpc`: `miner.rs:302` passes `prep.uncles.clone()`, while `mm_rpc` passes an empty vector — and the stratum path already has its uncles to hand, loaded at `:665-668` from the template, so the port should pass those rather than discard them **CLOSED 2026-09-22** (`e252e4f35c`): `stratum_submit`'s `CanonicalExtension` arm now calls `broadcast_block(&self.p2p_handler.p2p, block.clone(), uncles.clone())`, mirroring `miner.rs` rather than `mm_rpc` so the uncles travel with the block. Verified by `cargo test --release -p dwowd --all-features merge_mined` → 3 passed, 0 failed — the same run that carries the `OBL-C67` seating test, so the acceptance path and both of its new behaviours are exercised together. |
| OBL-C36 | **SATISFIED** | No serialization fallback substitutes a zero vector for a dedup hash: a `vec![0u8; 32]` on failure makes duplicate competing blocks acceptable and valid ones droppable | `H-C4` | C | **SATISFIED 2026-09-22 — the evidence is named in the "Verified 2026-09-22" section below; this marker makes that recorded verdict greppable rather than a fresh claim.** |
| OBL-C37 | **CLOSED** | `saturating_sub` on block timestamps does not mask a decreasing timestamp as a zero interval | `H-H2` | M | **Audited 2026-09-23 as PARTLY; CLOSED that same day — both routes out of the branch are now shut.** The audit was right that the 2026-09-22 change closed only the *decreasing* route, and right about the sign it left behind: `avg_interval == 0 → ratio_scaled = SCALE * 9 / 10` (`consensus.rs:237-239`) is *below* `SCALE`, and `BlockTarget::adjust` computes `current * scale / adjustment` (`sdk/src/blockchain.rs:338-353`), so `adjustment = 0.9·SCALE` and the target came back **11.1% higher** — *easier*, the opposite of the branch's own comment ("make it 10% harder"). Measured, not inferred: `0x00FFFFFF → 18_641_350 = 0x011C71C6`, inside the clamp, so it reached callers. **Two claims in that audit's own wording are corrected here.** (1) "a zero interval drags `avg_interval` down, `ratio_scaled` up, and the target easier" — the middle step has the wrong sign: a *lower* `avg_interval` raises `ratio_scaled`, which is the **harder** direction. The easing came from the all-zero branch's `SCALE * 9 / 10`, not from the average; the same wrong chain was written into the code's comment and is corrected there. (2) "the **equal**-timestamp route, *which the median rule permits*" — measured against `check_block_timestamp`: a timestamp is rejected iff at least six of the last eleven are ≥ it (`median = sorted[len/2]`, `len = 11`), so **at most six consecutive equal timestamps** pass, while the adjustment window is the last **ten**. A validated chain therefore always yields `avg_interval > 0`, and an all-zero window is **not reachable** by the route the audit named — the state is impossible, not fast. One route is left open and is stated rather than smoothed: `get_next_work_required` builds its timestamp vector by **skipping heights absent from the store** (`consensus.rs:405-410`), so two non-adjacent equal timestamps could form a length-2 window. That is not established to occur, and the repair does not depend on it. **The repair, in both paths and in lockstep** (`adjust_target` *and* `compute_adjustment`, or the same window would produce two different expected targets depending on the caller): an all-zero window **abandons the adjustment** — the target returns exactly as it was — matching what a decreasing pair already does, with the former branch and its comment **deleted rather than re-signed**, because there is no honest replacement: "instant blocks" would mean the *hardest* possible steering, which the window does not contain. **Control**: `test_compute_adjustment_all_zero_intervals_leaves_the_target_unchanged` asserts the target is unchanged and names the old value in its message, with a positive control that a window carrying *one* zero interval among real ones still steers; `test_adjust_target_all_zero_intervals_leaves_the_target_unchanged` is the same rule on the tracker path, which builds its window from `record_block` rather than from the store. **Negative-controlled**: restoring the old branch fails with `left: BlockTarget(18641350)` against `right: BlockTarget(16777215)`, recorded verbatim. **Consensus-affecting** — `get_next_work_required` is the authority a block's declared target is validated against — and **pin-neutral**: `src/linear/src/consensus.rs` is off the hashing and serialization path |

| OBL-C41 | **SATISFIED** | Mempool transactions are re-inserted on mining error paths in every miner, not only the RPC miner | `H-H7` | H | **SATISFIED 2026-09-22 — the evidence is named in the "Verified 2026-09-22" section below; this marker makes that recorded verdict greppable rather than a fresh claim.** |
| OBL-C42 | **ACCEPTED-WITH-REASON** | `take_competing_blocks()` follows the fallible work in all four call sites; it is destructive and currently precedes it in three | `H-H8` | M | **Re-verified 2026-09-22: one of the three production sites follows the rule; the other two precede it and compensate rather than reorder, which is the downgrade the "premises that moved" note records. The disposition — accepted with reason, no code change — is at the end of this cell.** The three production call sites are `lib.rs:1354`, `rpc/mm_rpc.rs:263` and `rpc/stratum.rs:191`. **`prepare_block` is the one that is right, and says why**: the take is annotated as *"the LAST step. `take_competing_blocks` is DESTRUCTIVE. All fallible operations (coinbase build, uncle-mint, fee_collect_tx) MUST succeed before this"* (`lib.rs:1348-1354`). The other two take first — `mm_rpc` takes at `:263` before building the template, `stratum` takes at `:191` before generating one — so the row's "precedes the fallible work" still describes them. **What changed is the consequence**: both now re-put on the failure path (`mm_rpc.rs:297`, `stratum.rs:221`, and `lib.rs:1709`/`:1837` on the miner's error paths), so a failed template no longer forfeits the competing blocks — the take is still premature, but it is no longer lossy, which is why this is a reordering question rather than a data-loss one. **The ordering this row prescribes is worse than the one the code has — read 2026-09-23, before the change was made, and the analysis restored here because an audit pass dropped it while keeping the conclusion.** The row says the take must *follow* the fallible work, and `prepare_block` is its model. Reading the model closely, the port would introduce a loss window that does not exist today: `prepare_block` peeks at `lib.rs:1222` and takes at `:1354`, with the coinbase build, uncle-mint and fee-collect between them — all potentially awaiting — and `take_competing_blocks` locks only `competing_blocks` + `competing_seen`, which `chain_state.rs:43` states outright: *"never `connect_lock`"*. So a competing block arriving during those steps is consumed by the take **without ever being built into an uncle** — the exact loss `HAZOP #5` fixed — and nothing compensates for it, because no compensation is written on the success path. The take-first shape's exposure is the opposite one: only its *failure* path, where the compensating `put_competing_blocks` restores precisely what was taken. Applied to `mm_rpc.rs:263` and `stratum.rs:191`, the prescribed reorder would trade an exactly-closed window for an unclosed one. **The invariant that matters is therefore not "take last"** but *"nothing is destroyed before the work that needed it succeeded, and nothing is consumed without being used"* — which take-first plus the exact put satisfies, and which the reorder would break. No code change made, deliberately. Residual, stated rather than implied: if these paths are ever tightened, the fix is a single operation returning both what it consumed and what the uncles were built from, so the two cannot diverge. **Restated as ACCEPTED-WITH-REASON 2026-09-23**: the proposition this row was filed under — that the take follows the fallible work in all four sites — is **not met and will not be**, because the two sites that take first compensate exactly on their failure paths and the prescribed reorder would open a window they close. A row whose prescription the code has outgrown is accepted with its reason rather than left PARTLY, which would read as work remaining. What stands in place of the original proposition is the invariant above, and the residual names the shape any future tightening should take; the register keeps no code change here by decision, not by omission |

| OBL-C43 | **CLOSED** | Concurrent stratum logins cannot observe a mismatched template+config pair | `H-H9` | M | **OPEN — the pair is written and read under two separate locks, with nothing pairing them. Read 2026-09-22.** `stratum_login` stores the two values in sequence under distinct locks — `*current_linear_template.lock().await = Some(template.clone())` then `*linear_recipient_config.lock().await = Some(config)` (`bin/dwowd/src/rpc/stratum.rs:228`, `:230`) — where `config` is the **login's** own config (it is what `generate_linear_block_template` was called with at `:205`) and the template embeds it. Two concurrent logins can interleave those two writes, leaving one login's template beside the other's config. Nothing detects it: `stratum_submit` reads them separately too (`:465`, `:581`, `:665`, `:704`), there is no generation counter, no joint struct, and no cross-check that the stored template's recipient matches the stored config. The impact is bounded by the design rather than by a guard — there is exactly one stored template and one stored config for the node, so a second miner's login overwrites the first's values and *any* submit is already validated against the newest login — but a torn pair means a submitted block judged against a recipient config its template was not built from. Closing it means storing the template and its config as one lock-protected value, not adding a check. **CLOSED 2026-09-23 by exactly that, in the form the row names.** The three fields are now one: `ActiveTemplate { template, height, recipient_config }`, under one lock, written **only** by `MiningState::store_template` — verified by grep, since that is the whole guarantee (there is no read-time check and none is possible: a torn pair is no longer an expressible state). The recipient is deliberately not an `Option`: a published template always has one, so "template without recipient" cannot be typed. Both submit handlers now take **one snapshot per submission** and read the template, its height, the per-block anchor key, the uncles and the recipient from that clone — the stratum handler previously acquired the lock three times (`:465`, `:590`, `:675`) and this row's own evidence named the consequence: its "the live template moved between login and submit" branch existed only to report the race the bundle removes, and is now unreachable from that handler, kept as a guard with its comment corrected. **Two consequences, both named rather than discovered later.** (i) The merge-mining login used to store the template and the height and **drop** the recipient it had just resolved, so the submit path's next-round template regeneration — guarded on "some config has been stored" — only ever fired on a node that had also served a stratum login; the recipient now travels with the template and the regeneration always runs. (ii) The stale-template check no longer carries its `template_h != 0` escape, because that guard existed for an atomic that read 0 before any template was stored and a snapshot cannot exist without a real height. **Control**: `active_template_tests::test_store_template_publishes_the_triple_as_one_value` publishes a triple, then a *different* triple at a different height with a different per-block recipient, and asserts no field of the first survives — the assertion a publication done in parts fails. **Negative-controlled** by publishing the second triple with the first's recipient retained, which is the old shape's defect class: it fails with two distinct `MiningRecipient` public keys, `0x3f7a2dc6…` against `0x2346b6b9…`, recorded. Also `rpc::mm_rpc` 9/9 green. Pin-neutral: `bin/dwowd` only |
| OBL-C44 | **CLOSED** | A reorg re-admits the disconnected block's transactions to the mempool, as Bitcoin Core does | `H-H10` | M | **OPEN — confirmed by the absence of the operation rather than by reading a broken one. Read 2026-09-22.** The mempool is told about inclusion at five acceptance sites — `bin/dwowd/src/lib.rs:1809`, `rpc/miner.rs:271`, `rpc/mm_rpc.rs:742`, `rpc/stratum.rs:699` and `proto/linear_broadcast.rs:447`, each calling `mp.mark_mined(&tx_hashes)` — and `mark_mined` (`crates/dwow-mempool/src/lib.rs:562-580`) removes those hashes from the pool, persistently (`sled remove`). There is **no inverse**: no `mark_unmined`, no re-insertion path, and neither the reorg driver (`bin/dwowd/src/task/consensus_linear.rs` — which does not mention the mempool at all) nor `CChainState::disconnect_block` (`chain_state.rs`'s only mempool mention is a doc comment on the nullifier set) puts a displaced block's transactions back. So a reorged-away transaction is dropped from the pool and stays gone until its own submitter re-broadcasts it; nothing in the node recovers it. That is a liveness difference from Bitcoin Core — which the row explicitly benchmarks against — rather than a safety one: no double-spend follows, since the nullifier gate is untouched and the transaction remains valid to re-submit. The wire to close it already exists (`mark_mined`'s hashes are computed at each site from `block.transactions`), but the re-admission must happen where the reorg is decided, and that is the daemon's `reorg_to_heavier_chain`, which currently has no mempool handle. **CLOSED 2026-09-23 at the site the row names, by giving it one.** `reorg_to_heavier_chain` and `activate_best_chain` now take `mempool: Option<&MempoolPtr>`; the re-admission happens in `activate_best_chain` **after** the reconnect, so every failure path above it leaves no trace of having been attempted — and the disconnected blocks were already collected there (stashed so a failed reconnect could be logged), which is what makes one location enough. The exclusion set is the row's implicit one made explicit: a displaced block's **coinbase** and **fee collection** are returned to nobody, because both are generated by the block that carried them (the coinbase pays its reward, FeeCollectV1 claims its fee pot and closes its commitment merkle tree). `Transaction::is_fee_collect_tx` is the second half of that, mirroring the existing `is_pow_reward_coinbase_tx` and carrying the same pinned cross-crate note for selector 0x06. **The one design decision worth recording**: a mempool parameter cannot be defaulted in Rust, so the alternative to what landed was threading it through 39 call sites — all of them tests and genesis, none of which has a mempool to give. The path is therefore split in name rather than widened: `accept_block_with_mempool` is the body, `accept_block` is `Some` body called with `None`, both in `block_acceptor.rs`, so no acceptance rule exists twice and each call site says whether it has a handle. The five production entry points — built-in miner, stratum, mm_rpc, miner_rpc and the sync loop's two accept paths — pass one; the P2P broadcast path passes an explicit `None` with the reason stated at the site, and genesis likewise (no mempool exists at genesis). **Control**: `transaction::tests::test_block_generated_tx_classification` pins the exclusion set in both directions (each selector classifies its own call and not the other; neither fires for another contract; neither fires for an empty call list). **Not covered, stated rather than implied**: the re-admission itself has no test — it needs a reorg harness carrying a mempool-admissible *non-coinbase* transaction, and the two halves of that fixture live in different places (`daemon_sync_integration`'s reorg tests build blocks whose only transaction is the coinbase; `mempool_tests.rs` has the transaction fixture but no reorg). Naming it is the honest form of that gap: what is verified is the rule and the call graph, what is not is the runtime effect. The call graph *is* exercised end to end — `daemon_sync_integration` **10 passed / 0 failed** including the three reorg tests, which drive `activate_best_chain` and reorg-to-heavier-chain with `None` and so prove the plumbing did not disturb the reorg — but those runs take the `None` branch, which is exactly the branch that re-admits nothing. Verified with the change: `dwow_chain` 279 passed / 0 failed including the new predicate test, and `dwowd`'s `rpc::`, `mempool_tests` and `active_template_tests` 20 passed / 0 failed |
| OBL-C45 | **PARTLY** | The contracts tree and the supply-chain tree reconcile automatically; no `verify_cumulative_supply()` exists and the `get_cumulative_supply` RPC returns stored state unverified | `H-H11` | M | **OPEN as recorded, with one half of its premise narrowed by what is already enforced. Read 2026-09-22.** The RPC half is exact: `blockchain_get_cumulative_supply` (`bin/dwowd/src/rpc/blockchain.rs:357-380`) reads `chain.supply_chain.get_latest()` — the *stored* entry — and returns its `value_commit`, `blind` and `total_supply` with no recomputation, carrying its own `UNVERIFIED(F3-1): needs cargo test -p dwowd --lib && ./verify_cumulative_supply.sh against a devnet node` tag. No `verify_cumulative_supply()` function exists in the tree at all; `verify_cumulative_supply.sh` is a script nobody calls at runtime, and it compares the stored *plaintext* total against the schedule while disclaiming the commitment half itself (`:70-72`). **What is already enforced, and why the row's first clause is too strong as written**: the two trees are not reconciled globally, but the supply-chain entry is *re-derived and cross-checked at every block* before it is written — `read_cumulative_from_overlay` recomputes `S_H = S_{H-1} + C_base` and rejects the block on any disagreement with the WASM overlay (`block_acceptor.rs:656-689`, `OBL-C25`), and `connect_block` is the only insertion path. So the residual is narrower than "unverified": there is no *read-time* verification and no end-to-end check against the emission schedule outside a script, but every write is step-verified. **PARTLY 2026-09-23: the read-time half is closed, the tree-vs-tree half is not.** `blockchain_get_cumulative_supply` now recomputes the schedule-side total at read time and **fails closed** on a mismatch — the error names both values, rather than returning an `UNVERIFIED` tag beside a `total_supply` a caller cannot distinguish from a verified one. The recomputation is `CumulativeSupplyChain::expected_cumulative_supply`, deliberately the *same name and definition* as `sim/crypto.py:expected_cumulative_supply` and `contrib/model/supply_chain_model.py` — the specification — which is also what `verify_cumulative_supply.sh` compared from the outside, in Python. It is O(height) in reward evaluations, so an operator RPC call and not a per-block path, and it is not memoised: a cache here would be a second piece of stored state to reconcile. **Control**: `test_supply_chain_audit` now cross-checks the schedule-side function against its own store-side audit at every height 1..5 — two independent sums, one over the rewards written into blocks and one over `expected_reward` from the schedule — so an off-by-one that started the sum at 0 or at `h+1` fails there instead of silently mis-reporting supply. The behaviour change is caller-visible and is named rather than implied: a node whose stored total disagrees with the schedule now returns a JSON-RPC **error** where it used to return data, and `verify_cumulative_supply.sh` already handles that branch. What stays open is the row's first clause: the contracts tree and the supply-chain tree are still not reconciled *against each other* anywhere, in Rust or in the script, and nothing in this fix pretends otherwise **— and that first clause now has a *statement*, which is what it was missing.** `proofs/lean/src/DarkFi/Consensus/SupplyReconciliation.lean` states the reconciliation as a composition of three models the layer already had, rather than as a fourth model: `MassBalance.lean`'s `Balanced` (the non-coinbase calls' Pedersen equation), `CoinbaseSplit.lean`'s `accepts` (the coinbase's five equations, whose `accepts_spendable_total` says the miner's note and the uncle notes *together* are the schedule's value — the pins are a share of the same reward, not a second issuance beside it), and `FeeCollect.lean`'s `rule` (the block's fee-collect shape). **The identity**: a block's *net creation of commitment value* — its issuance plus what its non-coinbase calls create, minus what they consume — is the schedule's value at that height (`creation_is_the_reward`), and summed over a chain it is `Σ expected_reward(H)` (`cumulative_creation_is_the_cumulative_supply`), which is the sum the supply-chain tree's own cumulative entry accumulates. **And the fee leg is load-bearing rather than decorative**: `non_coinbase_net_is_the_fee_aggregate` shows that `Balanced` alone fixes the block's net creation at `issuance − Σ fees` — the fees are *parked*, not destroyed — so the identity needs the fee calls to park nothing, and `parked_fees_break_the_identity` is the witness: 150 in against 100 out with a 50-unit aggregate, every value check in both models passing, the contracts tree growing by `base − 50` where the schedule says `base`. **What that exposes is the third leg's weakness, and it is this unit's residue**: `FeeCollect`'s rule is written over FeeV3 *call counts* where `MassBalance` carries the fee *amounts*, so the step from "this block parked an aggregate" to "this block collects" is a bridge **no code check ties**, taken as an explicit hypothesis in `a_block_with_fees_collects` and named there rather than hidden. **Non-vacuity is built from the modules' own witnesses** rather than from new data: `reconciliation_witness` evaluates the identity at `CoinbaseSplit.legalSplit` — that module's accepted split, reused rather than rebuilt — against a call-empty block. **What is unchanged is this row's first clause**: no Rust and no script reads the contracts tree against the supply chain, so `PARTLY` stands, and what moved is that a later reconciliation would be a check *of* a stated property rather than of a paragraph. The level is the specification's (`Commitment` is an integer pair, as in `MassBalance.lean`, so the identity is about values and blinds as integers rather than about `pallas::Point`), and the pot's arithmetic — that the collection's *value* is the aggregate the calls parked — remains the WASM check `FeeCollect`'s note records rather than a hypothesis this module takes |
