and the fixture's inputs satisfy the constraints the exec phase could see (the plan, the child's payment, the value-commitment comparison). *What is not known*: whether the proof is unsatisfiable — the client's witnesses disagreeing with the circuit's constraints, where `Proof::create` returns bytes regardless — or satisfiable but verified against other instances. *The next step is the instrument, not a guess*: `client_proof_self_verification.rs`'s shape, an owned test that builds this proof through the client and verifies it against the same `subscribe.zk.bin` the contract embeds, with `to_vec()`'s instances and no host involved. It runs in seconds where the heavyweight run takes two minutes, and it answers the question above outright. **PARTLY 2026-09-24: the instrument exists, and it has moved the failure twice — to an instance value, with every constraint excluded.** *The test* (`src/contract/subscription/tests/subscribe_proof_self_verification.rs`) builds the proof through the client with the fixture's values and verifies it against the **same `subscribe.zk.bin` the contract embeds**, with `to_vec()`'s three instances and no host involved; it runs in ~37 seconds against the heavyweight run's two minutes. *Measured*: `InvalidProof` — the client's proof does not verify against its own circuit with its own public inputs, so the defect is **below the metadata** and the alignment finding (3, 3, 3) is cleared rather than suspected. *Then the bisect*, each step a `zkas` recompile and a 40-second run: the circuit's four `constrain_equal_base` equalities were disabled one at a time and then all four at once, and **the proof still fails with every equality disabled** — so the disagreement is in an **instance value** (`tx_binding`, `tx_nonce` or `derived_id`), not in a constraint. *What that rules out, each read*: the client's witness vector matches the circuit's fifteen-witness declaration entry for entry, `value_blind` included as the one `Scalar`, so the slots are aligned; the client's `tx_binding_of` uses `DRK_POSEIDON_DOMAIN_TX_BINDING` = 3 (`sdk/src/crypto/constants.rs:57`) against the circuit's `DOMAIN_TX_BINDING = witness_base(3)`, and the PN circuits' working proofs settle that reading of `witness_base` as the literal 3; and the id the test passes is `Subscription::derive_id`, the derivation `OBL-C75` aligned to this circuit's own. *The next probe, stated so it is not re-derived*: instance **one** value — let the circuit `constrain_instance` only `tx_nonce` and have `to_vec()` return only that — and re-run the same test. A pass isolates the disagreement to `tx_binding` or `derived_id`; a failure says even a single witness-passthrough instance does not verify, which would put the defect in how this circuit's instances are verified at all rather than in one of them. *Not done here*: budget, not method — and the test is committed as a characterization test in the sense `OBL-C68`'s are,  **Then four probes, and three of them are negative in the informative direction.** (i) *The machinery, isolated*: a throwaway two-witness circuit compiled by the same `zkas` and proved and verified by the same `Proof::create`/`verify_zkp` pair **passes** — the proving path is sound, so the failure is inside this circuit. (ii) *The declaration shape*: a probe declaring exactly what this circuit declares — three constants (`EcFixedPointBase`, `EcFixedPointShort`, `EcFixedPoint`) and a `Scalar` witness among `Base`s — and indexing only through its single instance, also **passes**; and a probe declaring four witnesses while using two passes as well, which rules out any pruning of unused witnesses. That last one had to be excluded rather than waved away, because the client's fifteenth witness (`tx_binding`) is *shadowed* by the circuit's assignment to that name — the same shape the PN client uses, and the PN circuits verify. (iii) *The body*: the subscribe circuit **stripped to nothing but** `tx_binding = poseidon_hash(DOMAIN_TX_BINDING, tx_commitment, tx_nonce)` and its one instance, with the client's `to_vec()` reduced to that instance and the four equalities removed, **still fails**. So every constraint in the body is excluded, every instance *value* is excluded, the witness alignment is read and correct, the declaration shape is excluded, the machinery is excluded — and a forced `cargo clean -p` excludes a stale artifact, which is a trap this crate has already set once (`OBL-C75`). *The last fork, named so it is not re-derived*: build the witness vector **inside the test** rather than through `create_subscribe_proof` — same `zkbin`, same single instance, same eight `Base`s the stripped circuit reads — and run it. A pass says the client's vector is the defect; a failure says the compiled artifact is, and at that point the question is what `zkas` emits for *this* file rather than what any Rust in the tree does. **CLOSED 2026-09-24: the fork found it, and it is one line — `OBL-C78`'s own rule, in the client that did not follow it.** *The fork resolved it*: a witness vector assembled **inside the test** in the circuit's declared order, with the same zkbin and the same three instances, **verifies**, while the client's own path does not — so the defect is in the client's vector, and the difference is its last entry. `subscribe.rs` passed `Witness::Base(Value::known(pallas::Base::zero()))` under the comment `// tx_binding`, on the reading that the circuit's `tx_binding = poseidon_hash(DOMAIN_TX_BINDING, tx_commitment, tx_nonce)` **shadows** the declared witness and its value is never read. It is read: the assignment *constrains* the witness to equal the hash, so a zero there is a constraint no proof can satisfy — and because `Proof::create` returns bytes for an unsatisfied circuit, it surfaced as `invalid proof: call[1] namespace 'SubscribeV2'` at the L2 verify and as nothing the exec phase could see. The register states the rule in `OBL-C78`'s proposition, in these words: *the tx pair is `poseidon_hash([3, tx_commitment, tx_nonce])` and is never a literal zero*. The promissory_note clients follow it — `revoke.rs` and `transfer.rs` pass the computed value under the comment "shadowed, recomputed in-circuit" — and this was the client that did not. *The fix is that value*: `super::tx_binding_of(&self.tx_commitment, &self.tx_nonce)`, and the same change in this contract's `verify_access.rs` and `update_usage.rs`, which the same measurement convicted (three of its clients, one class). *Measured after it*: the self-verification file passes 3/3 — including a counts test added for the occasion, which **measures** that the client produces fifteen witnesses and three instances rather than assuming it — and the heavyweight run **accepts the subscribe** (`[subscription::subscribe_apply_v1] Subscription stored`, block committed) and then **grants access** (`[subscription::verify_access_v1] Access granted`), which is `OBL-C84`'s host check passing end to end for the first time. *What the row cost and what it leaves*: four probes and a 40-second test loop to localise what a two-minute pipeline run could only report; and the class is wider than this contract — see `OBL-C78`'s row, where the remaining sites are named, and `OBL-C108` for the file that looked like a site and is not. |
| OBL-C108 | **CLOSED** | A client module's circuit exists, and something reaches the module | `src/contract/subscription/src/client/rate_limit.rs` — **the module this row removed**, cited as the state found: the file and its `pub mod rate_limit;` declaration are both gone, which *is* the closure — and `src/contract/subscription/proof/` (no `rate_limit.zk`, which is why) | found 2026-09-24 measuring `OBL-C78`'s literal-zero class, where this file appeared as a candidate site | M | **NEW 2026-09-24: a client for a circuit that does not exist, referenced by nothing.** `client/rate_limit.rs` builds an `expected_capability`-shaped proof for a rate-limit circuit: it has a `to_witnesses` in a declared order, a `to_vec()`, and — until this measurement — the same literal-zero `tx_binding` line that is a genuine defect in three sibling files. What it does not have is a circuit: `src/contract/subscription/proof/` holds `cancel`, `renew`, `subscribe`, `update_usage` and `verify_access`, and no `rate_limit`; the contract's function enum has no `RateLimit` variant either, so no arm could call it. The module is declared (`client/mod.rs:30`) and referenced by nothing — not the harness, not the spec, not the other clients. *Why it is filed rather than deleted here*: the finding is not only the dead module but the mistake it nearly caused — the first fix applied to it was **unjustified**, a comment explaining a mechanism (`tx_binding` is assigned the computed hash and that constrains it) that its circuit cannot confirm, because there is no circuit. **CLOSED 2026-09-24: the module and its declaration are gone, and the measurement that made deletion right is the one that says nothing could have used it.** `client/rate_limit.rs` and `client/mod.rs`'s `pub mod rate_limit;` are removed. Measured before removing them: no `RateLimitPublicInputs`, `RateLimitCallData` or `create_rate_limit_proof` appears anywhere in `src/` or `bin/` outside the module itself, and `src/contract/subscription/proof/` holds exactly five circuits — `cancel`, `renew`, `subscribe`, `update_usage`, `verify_access` — with no `rate_limit.zk`, so it could not have been proved against anything. The crate compiles and its suite is green with it gone (29 + 3 + 2), and the artifact is rebuilt. *What the row keeps beyond the deletion*: the first edit made to this file was **unjustified** — a comment asserting that its `tx_binding` witness is constrained by the computed hash, which its circuit cannot confirm because there is no circuit. That edit was reverted, the deletion replaced it, and the lesson is cheap to state: a client in this tree should be read with its circuit beside it, because this one lived in a `pub mod` and referenced by nothing for as long as it did. |
| OBL-C113 | **OPEN** | The authority one subscription operation consumes is not the authority another has not yet used — a recurring payment can be paid again | `src/contract/subscription/proof/cancel.zk:13` and `renew.zk:13` (the same derivation), `src/contract/subscription/src/entrypoint.rs` (`cancel_v1`, `renew_v1` and their applies), `src/contract/subscription/src/model/mod.rs` (`nullifier_of`) | measured 2026-09-24 writing `OBL-C106`'s clients, where the fixture's two endpoints could not both be accepted | M | **NEW 2026-09-24: `cancel` and `renew` present the same nullifier, so whichever runs first consumes the other's right — and a subscription that can be renewed only once is not a subscription.** `cancel.zk:13` and `renew.zk:13` are the *same* `constrain_equal_base` — `computed_nullifier = poseidon_hash(DOMAIN_NULLIFIER, subscription_id, subscriber_secret)` — and both arms check that value against the spent set before marking it (`cancel_v1`, `renew_v1`; `cancel_apply_v1`, `renew_apply_v1`). *Measured, both orders, in the fixture's two runs*: cancel-then-renew is refused with `DrainProtection`-style `Custom(3)` — *"Subscription not active"*, the state check, reached before the nullifier one — and renew-then-cancel with `Custom(7)`, *"Nullifier already spent"* (`[subscription::cancel_v1] ERROR: Nullifier already spent`, on both chains of the final run). Either way **one of the two is refused because the other happened**, which is the row. *The harm, at the strength the evidence supports*: the nullifier is deterministic in `(id, secret)` and nothing re-seeds it, so a subscriber can renew once — the recurring payment a subscription exists for cannot recur — and a cancellation consumes the renew right. For a bearer instrument that is a defensible reading; for a plan sold by the period it is the product failing to work. *Repair, and it is a design decision rather than a fix*: either a per-operation nullifier (`poseidon_hash([DOMAIN, id, secret, operation])`, which keeps `cancel` and `renew` independent while keeping each single-use) or a re-seeding counter in the record so a renewal derives a fresh one. Whoever owns the subscription model decides; the row exists so the decision is made rather than discovered. `under` `OBL-C106`'s work the limitation is now visible: the fixture declares `cancel_after_renew` as an expected rejection instead of hiding the order dependency in an endpoint sequence. |
| OBL-C112 | **CLOSED** | A struct's fields are carried by every codec of that struct — a field added to three and not the fourth is dropped by the fourth, and the failure it produces is a panic rather than a decode error | `src/contract/subscription/src/model/mod.rs` (`Plan`: the sync `Encodable`/`Decodable` pair at `:97`/`:135`, the async pair, and the ρ-calculus pair in `impl Plan`) | measured 2026-09-24 working `OBL-C105`, whose new fields landed in three of the four | M | **NEW 2026-09-24: one struct, four codecs, and the field set is only as complete as the least complete one.** `Plan` is encoded and decoded four ways — a `dwow_serial::Encodable`/`Decodable` pair, an `AsyncEncodable`/`AsyncDecodable` pair, and the model's own slice `encode`/`decode`, which is the one the contract and the client actually call. Adding `uses_allowed`/`rate_period` for `OBL-C105` reached three of them; the model's `encode` kept writing 99 bytes while its `decode` expected 115, and the L2/exec run died with `slice_index_fail` **inside `Plan::decode`** — a wasm panic, not a `ContractError`, so the contract's own error path never sees this class at all. *Why it is filed rather than just fixed*: the shape recurs wherever a model type carries a hand-written codec (this crate has several, and `Subscription`'s five-field rate-limit block is in the same file), the compiler enforces nothing across the four, and the failure mode is a panic a bounded reader would not attribute to a missing `extend_from_slice`. **CLOSED 2026-09-24 — the repair is the first of the two directions, and the sweep that scoped it found the class smaller than this row claimed.** *What was done*: `Plan` has one codec. The slice `encode`/`decode` pair is the layout; the sync `Encodable`/`Decodable` pair and the async pair are now adapters over it — the encoders write `self.encode()`, the decoders read the fixed prefix, read the optional bulla and the allowance as the prefix's presence byte declares, and hand the bytes to `Plan::decode`. A field added to `Plan` can therefore reach one function and no other. *And the guard that made the failure a panic rather than an error*: `Plan::decode` reads the presence byte first and then compares the buffer length **exactly**, as `RenewParamsV1::decode` already did for its merkle proof; `MIN_ENCODED_SIZE` is derived from the layout constants (`FIXED_PREFIX 99 + ALLOWANCE 16`) instead of a literal, so it cannot drift from what `encode` writes. *The same shape, same file*: `Subscription::decode` guarded with a bare `MIN_ENCODED_SIZE` (264) while its two presence bytes make the real length 264/296/328 — repaired the same way. *The control, and it was run red first*: `src/contract/subscription/tests/codec_agreement.rs`, eight tests — the declared lengths for both structs in all their shapes, byte-for-byte agreement between the slice and serial codecs, a two-plan stream that must be consumed exactly, and four buffers that must be refused. Against the pre-repair guards (measured by restoring `MIN_ENCODED_SIZE = 99` / `264` and the minimum guard, running the refusal tests, then re-applying) all three refusal cases **panicked** instead of erroring: `range end index 107 out of range for slice of length 99` (`Plan::decode`), `range end index 131 out of range for slice of length 115` (a `Plan` whose flag claimed a bulla it did not carry), `range end index 296 out of range for slice of length 264` (`Subscription::decode`). With the repair they return `ContractError::IoError` naming the expected length. `test_heavyweight_subscription` **passes** (386 s) on the rebuilt artifact, so the stricter guards hold on the wasm path a real `subscribe` takes. *Why the existing tests did not catch the original*: `integration.rs`'s `test_plan_encoding` round-trips `serialize`/`deserialize` — the serial pair, one of the four homes — so a defect in the slice pair, the one the contract calls, was invisible to it. *And this row's own claim, corrected twice*: (a) `MIN_ENCODED_SIZE` for `Plan` was **99 on the tree**, not the 115 `OBL-C105`'s row records as landed — the length guard was therefore still the pre-allowance one, and the 99-byte case above is that guard letting an old-format buffer through to the reads; the C105 row is corrected in place. (b) "the shape recurs" is now measured rather than asserted: a sweep of all **322** `impl dwow_serial::Encodable` bodies in `src/` finds **313 delegating** and **5 laying their own fields out** — `ExternalChainProof`, `XmrDepositProof`, `ZcashDepositProof`, `AztecDepositProof` and `LitecoinDepositProof`, all in `bridge/src/model/mod.rs`, each with **no inherent slice codec** and so a single home rather than two. With `Plan` repaired there is **no two-codec struct in `src/`** (instrument: /tmp/measure_dual_codecs.py, which reports `Plan` as a duplicate before the repair and not after). A guard-shape sweep over the 615 slice decoders in `src/` finds **402 checking the length exactly and 0 using a `MIN_ENCODED_SIZE` minimum**; the 2 minimum-guard sites at `HEAD` were these two, and the sweep's instrument was controlled by running it against both the `HEAD` and the repaired text of the file (7 exact / 2 minimum → 8 exact / 0 minimum). The remaining **213** slice decoders guard some other way — a literal minimum, or per-field bounds checks — and whether any of those fails the same way is **not measured** here. *What is owed*: the async pair is an adapter now but **no test executes it** — the crate declares no dev-dependencies and the async traits are `#[cfg(feature = "client")]`, so a round-trip needs an executor this crate would have to take on. Its correctness rests on being three lines around `Plan::encode`/`Plan::decode` and on the sync path's tests; a wallet-path exercise of it is the missing half. |
| OBL-C109 | **FAILS** | The specification's maturity rule is the code's — the same source of truth, keyed the same way | `contrib/model/chain_model.py` (`is_commitment_mature`); `src/linear/src/chain_state.rs` (`check_coinbase_maturity`) | found 2026-09-24 while modelling the chain-level commitment set in `proofs/lean/src/DarkFi/Consensus/CommitmentSet.lean` | M | **NEW 2026-09-24, and the defect is a *citation to a function that does not exist* plus a keyed-the-wrong-way model.** `chain_model.py`'s `is_commitment_mature` carries the docstring "Mirrors `src/linear/src/chain_state.rs:is_commitment_mature()`" — and **no such function exists**. The Rust's maturity rule is `check_coinbase_maturity`, and it keys by **nullifier** (`nullifier_set : BTreeMap<Nullifier, BlockHeight>`); the Python keys by **commitment** (`commitment_set: dict  # commitment → creation_height`). That is not a naming slip: the Rust's own docstring records the commitment-set route as one that "`P2-9` Item 4 considered and rejected", because a commitment-set lookup "would be a second source of truth for maturity (hazid `RC5` class)" — and the rejection is well-founded for a reason this campaign then measured: `commitment_set` is **pruned at the maturity window** (`retain(\|_, h\| *h >= prune_h)`, the same line that prunes `nullifier_set`), so it cannot answer "how old is this?" for anything old enough to matter, and a pruned entry is indistinguishable from one never recorded. So the specification models the design the code rejected and cites a function that was never written to justify it. **What is *not* wrong**: the rule's *shape* — both models refuse a commitment younger than `COINBASE_MATURITY` blocks, and the Python's arithmetic (`current_height - created_at >= COINBASE_MATURITY`) is the rule. The defect is the source of truth and the citation, and it is the class `OBL-C29` exists to keep honest ("the Python model *is* the specification"). **And a second measurement from the same unit, recorded here because it is what makes the defect's impact nil**: the chain-level `commitment_set` has **no production reader** — it is written at the coinbase and fee-collect paths, pruned, restored from sled on restart — and its only accessor, `has_commitment`, is called from `bin/dwowd/src/tests/daemon_sync_integration.rs`, whose assertions are about *reorg reversal* (corrected: `bin/dwowd/src/tests/uncle_minting.rs:260,274` calls it too — "no production reader" stands, both are tests). Uniqueness of commitments *is* enforced, but by the contracts' own per-contract trees (`db_contains_key(commitment_set, …)` at each exec), which are not this set. So there is no live harm: the spec is unfaithful about a structure nothing reads, and the register records it so the Tier 6 work inherits the finding rather than the model **READ 2026-09-24 from the model side, and the route is bigger than the row implies — which is worth knowing before someone starts it.** The citation is corrected in `contrib/model/chain_model.py`: its docstring no longer claims to mirror a function that does not exist, and now states the code's rule, its site, and the three differences. **What cannot be closed by editing that docstring is the keying, and the reason is a missing *domain* rather than a wrong choice**: the model's `Transaction` carries no `nullifiers` — the model's `Transaction` carries **no `nullifiers` field** (`chain_model.py:148-163` — the word occurs in the file only in the docstring this same change added, so the claim as first written contradicted itself) — and the model has **no spend path at all**, modelling minting, reorgs and reward arithmetic, while the code's rule is a block-level rule *over spends* (`for nullifier in &tx.nullifiers`, `chain_state.rs:696`). So "keyed the same way" is unreachable here until the model gains a spend dimension, which changes the specification's structures and is therefore a decision to take rather than a drive-by to land. **Two more divergences the same reading found, recorded here so they do not go down with the row's wording**: the model's predicate answers `False` for a commitment it does not know, which for a caller that refuses on `False` is a *stricter* rule than the code's "not this rule's business"; and the model never *applies* it — `is_commitment_mature` has no caller outside its own tests (`:1411-1432`, `:1468`) — whereas the code calls its rule from `connect_block`, so the specification states a rule it does not enforce, keyed by a set the code rejected for the reason this row already gives. **Status stays FAILS, deliberately**: the property's two live clauses are "the same source of truth" and "keyed the same way", neither holds, and correcting a citation satisfies neither — recording that as PARTLY would be closing a row by wording. Verified as a non-regression rather than as a fix: `python3 contrib/model/chain_model.py` → **15/15 passed** after the docstring change |
| OBL-C110 | **FIXED** | The specification's timestamp rule is the rule the code enforces — the same trigger range, the same checks, and no claim of absence | `contrib/model/chain_validation_model.py` (`validate_timestamp`); `src/linear/src/validation.rs` (`check_block_timestamp`) | found 2026-09-24 while modelling the rule in `proofs/lean/src/DarkFi/Consensus/BlockTimestamp.lean` | M | **NEW 2026-09-24, and it is three divergences rather than one — all measured, all in the same direction.** *(i) The specification skips the bootstrap window the code protects.* The Python applies the median check only `if len(recent_heights) >= 11`, so for blocks 2 through 11 the rule does not apply at all; the Rust guards on `height > GENESIS && !recent_timestamps.is_empty()` and takes *whatever* timestamps exist, with a comment giving the reason — "For blocks 2-11 this uses whatever timestamps exist (fewer than `MEDIAN_BLOCK_COUNT`), preventing difficulty/time manipulation during bootstrap." So the spec is **weaker than the code** over exactly the window the code's comment says protection is for. *(ii) The specification states as a rule what the code deliberately does not enforce.* Its docstring gives two clauses, the second a two-hour future-timestamp bound, and `validation.rs` says of that check: "The non-deterministic future-timestamp check is a P2P policy, not a consensus rule." A spec that lists a policy as consensus tells a reader the chain rejects something it accepts. *(iii) The specification says the rule is not implemented, which is false.* Its docstring ends "Not yet implemented in Rust." while `check_block_timestamp` is a consensus rule reached from `check_block_header`, with a test beside it — a statement about the code that the code contradicts, the `OBL-C111` species. **What is *not* wrong**: the rule's arithmetic. Both sort the window and both refuse `timestamp <= sorted[len / 2]`, so `sorted[len/2]`'s *upper*-median reading (the middle sample for the 11 the rule names, the upper of two middles for a shorter window) is shared. **Two things the unit did not establish, recorded so they are not read into it**: the rule's security claim — that an adversary controlling at most `len / 2` of the window cannot lower the floor — is *stated* in the model's note and not proved there (the order-statistics lemmas exist; the proof was not finished), and no concrete window is checked, because neither `mergeSort` nor `mergeSort'` reduces in the kernel — the first leaves `decide` stuck on a `beq`, the second trips instance search on a lambda relation. The rule's arithmetic is therefore pinned by the specification's own Python test and not by Lean, and the model claims only what it proves **FIXED 2026-09-24: the specification was corrected to the code and all three divergences above are closed.** *(i)* `validate_timestamp` now applies the median rule whenever the window is non-empty (`if recent_heights:`, replacing `if len(recent_heights) >= 11`), so heights 2–11 — the bootstrap window whose protection the code's own comment gives as the reason — are no longer exempt. *(ii)* The two-hour future bound is out of the rule and out of the docstring, which now states that the code does not enforce it and that no consensus rule can: a predicate reading the local clock is not a function of block data (`type-system.md` §9). *(iii)* "Not yet implemented in Rust" is replaced by the citation to `check_block_timestamp` and both call sites (`chain_state.rs:1093`, `:1691`). **Verified by the specification's own suite, and the new assertions are the negative control for each divergence**: `python3 contrib/model/chain_validation_model.py` → **41/41 passed**, where a timestamp at the median of the 2-, 3- and 11-sample windows is rejected and one above it accepted — the control asserting the window is shorter than 11 before it does so, so the case cannot silently become the 11-sample one — and a three-hour-ahead timestamp is **accepted**, with the test named for the reason. **The unit opened a row rather than only closing one, and that is the part worth carrying forward**: divergence *(ii)*'s policy is not merely un-enforced *by consensus*, it is un-enforced **anywhere** — the sentence in `validation.rs` that asserted its enforcement was itself the only trace of the concept in production code — so it is minted as `OBL-C120`, and that sentence was corrected in the same change rather than left asserting an enforcement that does not exist **APPENDED 2026-09-24, from this register's own adversarial review: `FIXED` is true of the rule's *content* and was silent about its *enforcement* — a gap `OBL-C109` records for the maturity rule, and one this row must not omit for the same property.** The model's `validate_block` never calls `validate_timestamp`: a grep for the name in `contrib/model/chain_validation_model.py` returns only its definition and its own test. The code, by contrast, reaches `check_block_timestamp` from `connect_block` (`chain_state.rs:1093`) and from `validate_competing_block` (`:1691`). So the specification states the rule and does not apply it, which is exactly what `OBL-C109` finds for maturity, and the two rows now say the same thing about the same kind of property. What is closed here is the rule's content and its citation — not the model's fidelity as a whole, and not the model's enforcement of anything |
| OBL-C111 | **FIXED** | A transaction is exempted from a check **only when it is the coinbase** — every accept-path exemption classifies with the position *and* the contract, never with the selector byte alone | `src/linear/src/execution.rs` (the L2 witness loop, `:618`); `bin/dwowd/src/lib.rs` (the miner's assembly filter); `bin/dwowd/src/rpc/tx.rs` (both predicates); the shared classifier `src/linear/src/transaction.rs` (`is_pow_reward_coinbase_tx`) | `scripts/check-coinbase-classifier.sh` (wired into `scripts/run-all-tests.sh`); `test_selector_only_probe_is_not_a_coinbase_classifier` (`transaction.rs`) | C | **FIXED 2026-09-24. Found by reading, not by a failing test, and the audit that found it was not looking for it.** Measured, both sides. **The defect:** the L2 loop in `execution.rs` — *the only place a transaction's ZK proofs are verified at block acceptance* — exempted a transaction on `tx.first_call_is_pow_reward()`, and that predicate (`transaction.rs:348`) matches `data[0] == 0x05` **against any contract**; its own docstring calls it "regardless of contract id … the structural variant used where the block-structure rule is checked separately from contract identity". **Around twenty contracts use 0x05 as a real function code** — `IdentityFunction::IssueCapabilityV1` (`identity/src/lib.rs:143`), `AttestationFunction::ConsumeClaimV1` (`attestation/src/lib.rs:84`), `dex`/`relayer_endowment` `UpdateConfigV1`, `escrow` `CancelV1`, `auction` `RefundBidV1`, `stablecoin` `RepayStableV1`, `promissory_note` `OtcSwapV1`, `drain_protection` `TransferV1`, `daos_escrow` `TreasurySpendV1`, `pool_stake` `SlashCoverageV1`, `bearer_bond` `BurnStakeV1`, `labor_market` `DisputeV1`, `lottery` `ExpireLotteryV1`, `insurance_market` `FileClaimV1`, `tender` `CancelTenderV1`, `darkbet_exchange` `SettleMarketV1`, `game_room` `CallV1`, `oracle` `SetOracleActiveV1`, `subscription` `DaoControlV1` — an exact extraction (`Variant = 0x05` across every contract's `lib.rs`), **20 non-native contracts**, 21 including `PoWRewardV1` itself — so a transaction whose **first** call is one of those, **at any index**, had its proofs never verified, and the `continue` bypasses both the verification and the `else` branch that rejects a tx with no accumulated metadata tables. A fabricated proof rode in; the mechanism is exactly the HAZOP-C1 class the verifier's own docstring claims to close ("a fabricated transaction has no valid proof and is rejected here"). **Why nothing compensated, measured:** `block_acceptor.rs:314`'s pre-witness loop *does* run for such a tx (it uses the correct classifier), but `verify_single_tx` is documented as the structural mempool-admission check whose proof-requiring list is native-token selectors only (`0x00\|0x02\|0x03\|0x04\|0x08`), so a non-native call needs no proof there; there is no WASM host function that verifies proofs (`src/runtime/import/` has none) and no contract verifies its own; and `verify_core_tx_with_tables`'s only production caller is that L2 loop. **Three sibling sites had the same defective classifier, and they were the tell**: `chain_state.rs::check_coinbase_maturity` carries the shared classifier *and states the rule in its docstring* ("through the shared classifier … rather than a re-derived predicate") — so the tree already knew the standard and two of its accept-path sites re-derived it wrongly instead. **Fixed at four sites, one root cause**: `execution.rs` now uses `tx_idx == 0 && tx.is_pow_reward_coinbase_tx()` (the index gate carried deliberately, since `execute_block` is `pub`, so the exemption states position *and* content and stays correct for a caller that has not run `validate_block_structure`); `lib.rs`'s miner-side assembly filter and both `rpc/tx.rs` predicates use the shared classifier — there the defect was **over-rejection** rather than a bypass (a legitimate `identity.0x05` tx could not be validated or submitted), and `lib.rs`'s site could at worst build a block `check_coinbase_maturity` then rejects, i.e. self-inflicted work — **with the configuration measured rather than assumed**: that enforcement lives in `CChainState::connect_block` (`chain_state.rs:891`), which is `#[cfg(feature = "pow")]`, and `check_coinbase_maturity` is private and called only from there (which is why a *non-`pow`* `cargo check` reports it dead — the third configuration artefact this session has had to read past). The production node builds with `pow`, so the compensation is real in the configuration that matters; in a build without it, `connect_block` does not exist and no block is accepted at all, so the site is moot rather than live. `chain_state.connect_block` is called from `block_acceptor.rs:543` and `:639`. **The residual, stated as an obstruction rather than an assurance:** there is no end-to-end regression test, because reproducing the bypass needs a *deployed* non-native contract whose 0x05 function can be called with a fabricated proof and whose call otherwise executes, and the accept-path fixtures in this tree top out at selector 0x04 (`promissory_note` `TransferV1`). So the protection is `scripts/check-coinbase-classifier.sh`, a source gate that fails on any use of the probe outside its definition that is not position-gated — falsified before landing (reintroducing the original line makes it exit 1 naming `execution.rs:618`, and restoring it exits 0) — plus `test_selector_only_probe_is_not_a_coinbase_classifier`, which pins the exact input the two classifiers disagree on. A source gate is weaker than a behavioural test and this row says so rather than presenting it as equivalent. **Cost of the fix, measured**: `src/linear` and `bin/dwowd` appear in **no** contract's `SOURCE_MANIFEST` (`scripts/check-artifact-freshness.sh`), so no contract artifact was invalidated and the genesis pin did not move. |
| OBL-C114 | **CLOSED** | Every slice `check_uncles` indexes by its loop index is length-checked — one guard per parallel slice, not one guard for two of them | `src/linear/src/validation.rs` (`check_uncles`, the `uncle_targets` guard and the `proofs[i]` index) | `scripts/check-coinbase-classifier.sh`'s sibling gate is *not* this — nothing checks it; found by reading, 2026-09-24 | L | **OPEN — a defence asymmetry, measured as not reachable from untrusted input, so recorded rather than repaired in the unit that found it.** `check_uncles` indexes two slices by one index: `uncle_targets[i]` and `proofs[i]`. It guards the **first** — `uncle_targets.len() != uncles.len()` returns `Err`, with the reason stated in its own comment ("Both slices are built from the same uncle set by the caller; a mismatch would silently skew every PoW verdict, so fail closed on it") — and does **not** guard the second, while `proofs[i]` is a slice index the loop reaches for every uncle. The same argument the comment gives for targets applies verbatim to proofs, which is what makes it an asymmetry rather than a design choice. **Reachability measured, 2026-09-24, and it is not reachable from untrusted input:** `UncleProof` appears nowhere outside `src/linear/src/` (`grep -rn UncleProof --include=*.rs src/ bin/` — the only non-`validation.rs` hits are its own definition in `block.rs`, its use in `verify_uncle_proof`, and `lib.rs`'s re-export), so it is never serialized and never decoded from the wire; its only constructor is `build_uncle_merkle`, which emits exactly `uncles.len()` proofs (`block.rs:574`, `(0..uncles.len())`); and the single production caller (`bin/dwowd/src/block_acceptor.rs:180`) passes that same vector. So the exposure is an unchecked index on a **`pub`** function's parameter — a contract a future or external caller can break, not a live defect, and the register says which it is rather than presenting it as either. Severity L for that reason: the failure mode is a panic on a mismatched slice, the `OBL-C71` class, and it needs a caller that does not exist today. The fix is one guard clause beside the one already there; **not taken here**, because the unit that found it was authorised for the L2 classifier defect and this is a different root cause. Modelled rather than merely described: `Consensus/UncleRules.lean`'s `guard_buys_every_index` and `unaligned_has_an_uncovered_index` state what the guard on the first slice buys and what its absence permits, so the property is a theorem even though the second guard is missing. **CLOSED 2026-09-24: the second guard is beside the first, and the asymmetry the row measured is gone.** *What was added*: `if proofs.len() != uncles.len()` returns `BlockIsInvalid("check_uncles: {n} uncles but {m} proofs")`, immediately after the `uncle_targets` guard, with the reason the row's own comment gives for that one ("both slices are built from the same uncle set by the caller") written into it — the row's whole argument was that the comment's reasoning applies verbatim to the slice it does not cover, and that is now what the code says. *The control, and its pre-fix half was measured rather than argued.* The test `check_uncles_rejects_misaligned_proof_slice` passes **two uncles, one proof, and a full target slice** — deliberately, so the case that reaches the second guard is one the first guard passes, which is what makes it this row's test rather than a repeat of `check_uncles_rejects_misaligned_target_slice`. Measured with the guard temporarily neutered: `thread 'validation::tests::check_uncles_rejects_misaligned_proof_slice' panicked at src/linear/src/validation.rs:353:48: index out of bounds: the len is 1 but the index is 1` — the `&proofs[i]` inside the `verify_uncle_proof` call, which is the site the row named. Guard restored: that test and the eleven other `check_uncles` tests pass, and the file's whole test module is green (`cargo test --release -p dwow_chain --lib --all-features validation::` → **28 passed, 0 failed**). *The Lean half needed less than the row implied, and that is the interesting part.* `Consensus/UncleRules.lean`'s `guard_buys_every_index` and `unaligned_has_an_uncovered_index` are stated over `{α β : Type}` — **any** two lists — so they were already the statement of the guard this row adds and no theorem was touched; `LEAN_NUM_THREADS=4 lake build DarkFi.Consensus.UncleRules` and a full `lake build` both succeed. What *was* stale was the prose around them: the module header said the asymmetry was "recorded rather than repaired" and "**not** modelled here — a model would be modelling a hazard rather than a rule", and Part 2's preamble said the guard on the first slice is what the theorems are *for*. Both paragraphs now state that both slices are guarded, that the theorem pair covered the second from the start, and that the reachability measurement below is what held the severity down rather than a reason to leave the gap — corrected to the code rather than left to mislead, the same way this register corrects a row whose premise a later measurement falsified. *Unchanged, because it was measured rather than assumed*: the reachability finding stands — `UncleProof` is never serialized or decoded from the wire and its only constructor emits exactly `uncles.len()` proofs — so this was always an API contract on a `pub` function, and L remains the honest severity of the *defect*, not of the repair. The value of the guard is that a future or external caller cannot break the contract, which is what a `pub` function's parameter list is for |
| OBL-C115 | **SATISFIED** | A block's declared PoW target is the one consensus requires at that height, so stage 1's comparison is against a value the block does not choose | `src/linear/src/validation.rs` (`check_block_header` stage 2; `check_pow_stage` stage 1); `src/sdk/src/blockchain.rs` (`BlockTarget::reached`) | Rust; `Consensus/BlockHeader.lean` | H | **SATISFIED, and the row is new — this obligation had no row until 2026-09-24, which is why the unit that modelled it minted one rather than appending.** The proposition is stage 2 of the two-stage rule, and it is the check the code's own docstring gives its reason to: "Stage 2: `block.header.target == expected_target` — target matches consensus rules (GetNextWorkRequired). This prevents self-declared-target attacks." **The mechanization states the attack as a mechanism rather than as a name:** `stage_one_is_monotone_in_the_declared_target` proves the stage-1 predicate is an **up-set** in the target the header supplies, so a producer who wants a block to pass declares a larger one, and `stage_one_admits_every_hash_at_the_maximum_target` is the extreme case where every hash in the domain passes. So stage 1 in isolation is not a difficulty rule at all — it is a comparison against a number the block chooses — and stage 2 is what makes it one. **And the pair is a redundancy, measured:** `two_stages_collapse` proves that stage 1 *for some* declared target plus stage 2 is exactly the consensus comparison, with the quantifier existential because the same statement with `declared` free is **false** (from `h ≤ expected` there is no `h ≤ declared`; stage 2 removes the freedom, and `declared_target_is_pinned` gives uniqueness). So the two-stage structure buys a **diagnostic** — which check failed — and not a second condition. **One modelling boundary recorded rather than glossed:** `hash_is_valid` is applied to a `u32` built from the hash's first four bytes, so the comparison's domain is `u32::MAX` and the work a target buys is capped by that domain; `Consensus/BlockHeader.lean` carries `MAX_TARGET` and claims nothing about the hash's remaining bytes. Not a defect — a boundary the model would otherwise silently widen by using `Nat`. **Negative control:** the code's `test_rejects_target_mismatch_above_genesis` and `test_accepts_matching_target_and_pow` are the two directions, and the model's `two_stages_collapse` is the statement they are about. |
| OBL-C116 | **SATISFIED** | The order of `check_block_header`'s checks is a diagnostic, not a rule: acceptance is invariant under any permutation of them | `src/linear/src/validation.rs` (`check_block_header`, and the module's own purity claim); `Consensus/BlockHeader.lean` (`order_is_diagnostic`) | `Consensus/BlockHeader.lean`; the code's own comment recording the reordering fix | L | **SATISFIED, and minted 2026-09-24 by the same unit as `OBL-C115`** — this is the second proposition of the pair that unit found had no row. **The claim the code makes, verbatim:** "Previous hash — fork detection MUST come before Stage 2 target. A block from a different fork will have the wrong previous_hash. Failing here with InvalidPreviousHash is the correct diagnostic. Previously this was checked AFTER Stage 2 target, causing fork blocks to fail with misleading 'target mismatch' errors." The comment is **right about the effect and only its emphasis is wrong**: the fix that records it changed *which error a fork block reports*, not whether the block is accepted. `order_is_diagnostic` proves that for any two permutations of a list of predicate checks, acceptance is equivalent — and the module's own purity claim ("Every function in this module is **pure** … No sled, no locks, no async, no side effects") is what makes the checks a conjunction rather than a sequence of effects, so the abstraction is faithful rather than convenient. Stated for `List.Perm` generally and not for the one swap the comment records, because the general form is the claim. Severity L deliberately: no acceptance is at stake, and the row exists because a comment that reads as a rule should be recorded as a diagnostic *in the register the comments are audited against* — this is the third row of the campaign whose subject is that a stated rule measures as something weaker. **Not a defect, and the row says so.** |
| OBL-C117 | **SATISFIED** | A block's fee-collection structure is admissible iff **at most one** `FeeCollectV1` call exists, the collection is present **iff** the block has fee calls, and it is the block's **final** transaction when present | `src/linear/src/validation.rs` (`validate_block_structure`, the call-count guard and the four-arm `match`) | Rust: the nine `phase05_*` tests; `Consensus/FeeCollect.lean` | H | **SATISFIED, and the row is new — this obligation had no row until 2026-09-24, the second unit running to mint a row for an absent proposition rather than append to a neighbour's.** **The table is a presentation of the rule, mechanized:** `codeVerdict` transcribes the guard and the four arms *in the code's own order*, and `codeVerdict_is_the_rule` proves it equivalent to the two clauses above — so the nested `match` is the rule, not a second rule, and the arms' order matters only for which message a refusal carries (the same relation `Consensus/BlockHeader.lean` found between `check_block_header`'s checks and their order, recorded as `OBL-C116`). **And the guard is load-bearing, not belt-and-braces:** the `match`'s third component — the collect **call** count — is `_` in all four arms, which reads as a component the table ignores; `arm_outcome_ignores_the_call_count` says exactly how far that goes (given the guard, the verdict does not depend on the count at all) and `call_count_guard_is_load_bearing` refutes the alternative with the witness the guard exists for: **two** collect calls, with fees present and the collection final, are accepted by the arms and refused only by the guard. So the component is inert because it has already been excluded, not because it does not matter. The Rust test `phase05_rejects_two_collect_calls_in_one_tx` constructs precisely that input, and the call-versus-transaction distinction is the test's point. **One proxy recorded rather than glossed:** the clause "present iff there are fee calls" is implemented over a **call count**, and the code's own comment says why — "FeeV3 fees are hidden behind Pedersen commitments — exact amounts are not available in call data. The structural validator checks fee presence, not sum." So the structural rule cannot see an amount, and a block carrying a zero-value FeeV3 call must still carry a collection; the proxy is strictly wider than "the fees sum to more than zero". That is a **prerequisite rather than a defect** — the pot's arithmetic is checked in WASM against the collection, and this layer's job is to make the block's shape well-formed first — and the model states the proxy *as* the predicate (`feeCalls` is a count, never an amount) so the boundary is in the type rather than in a footnote. **Coverage is complete and pre-existing:** the nine `phase05_*` tests give one negative control per arm, one for the guard, and two for detector precision (`phase05_short_fee_call_not_counted`, `phase05_ignores_other_contracts_zero_selector`), and the model's `arm_witness` names each arm's test in its own comment. |
| OBL-C118 | **SATISFIED** | A coinbase transaction carries **exactly one** contract call, and it is the pow reward — so the whole-transaction skips that follow it (mass balance, witness verification, pre-witness checks) each have a blind spot of size one, and it is the call the block is *for* | `src/linear/src/validation.rs` (`validate_block_structure`, the four opening structural checks); `proof_of_token_balance.rs` (the skip); `execution.rs` and `block_acceptor.rs` (their skips) | Rust: `rejects_compound_coinbase_two_calls`, `accepts_coinbase_with_single_call`; `Consensus/CoinbaseStructure.lean` | H | **SATISFIED, and the row is new — the third consecutive unit to mint rather than append, which is itself a finding about the register's coverage of `validation.rs`.** **The claim being checked is the code's own, verbatim:** "Coinbase tx MUST have exactly 1 contract call (PoWRewardV1 only). Extra calls in tx[0] would bypass Pedersen mass balance (`proof_of_token_balance` skips entire tx[0] when first call is PoWRewardV1), ZK witness verification (`execution.rs` skips entire tx[0]), and pre-witness checks (`block_acceptor.rs` skips entire tx[0]). Structural fix makes call-level skip fixes defense-in-depth." That comment is **right**, and the model proves its content rather than restating it. **The four checks collapse to one shape** (`wellFormed_shape`): non-empty, first call is the pow reward, exactly one coinbase-classified transaction, and exactly one call in it — together they say the block *is* `[powReward] :: rest`, with the count rule retained. **And the payoff is `full_coverage`:** for a structurally valid block the joined calls are the pow reward followed by exactly what the mass-balance rule accumulates, so the rule's blind spot has size one and it is the pow reward's own call. **`compound_coinbase_escapes` is the refutation**, stated so no weakening rescues it: drop the single-call rule and *some* call in `tx 0` is invisible to the balance rule, the two-call `tx 0` the comment describes being the witness — the input `rejects_compound_coinbase_two_calls` constructs. **So the comment's closing phrase reads the dependency backwards**: the structural rule is not a defence-in-depth duplicate of call-level fixes, it is the thing that gives the whole-transaction skips their soundness, and a call-level fix cannot substitute for it. **Two guards each make a later index safe**, stated with their refutations: the empty-block check precedes `transactions[0]`, and the single-call check precedes `contract_calls[0]` — the fourth site of the "guard whose purpose is an index" shape this campaign has found, and the first where nothing is missing. **And the uniqueness rule has a second use the comment does not mention:** `block_acceptor`'s pre-witness loop and `check_coinbase_maturity` exempt by classifier with **no index gate**, where `proof_of_token_balance` gates on `tx_idx == 0`. `gate_free_skip_exempts_nothing` proves the divergence harmless — on a structurally valid block a gate-free skip over the tail exempts nothing — which is the prose claim `Consensus/CoinbaseSplit.lean` makes about its own loop, now a theorem about all three sites. Worth a theorem precisely because the sites *look* inconsistent. **Header continuity is deliberately not modelled** (`height == current + 1`, `previous == prev`): two equalities with nothing to state, and a unit for them would have only completeness as its justification — recorded rather than left to look like an oversight. |
| OBL-C119 | **CLOSED** | A mint that would leave the pool below its configured minimum collateralization is refused — the property `MintStableParams` documents for itself and the four sibling circuits each enforce for their own arm | `src/contract/stablecoin/src/model/mod.rs:522` (the doc comment *"ZK proof: mint doesn't violate pool collateralization"*), `:516`/`:520` (`total_debt`/`total_collateral`, *"for ratio check"*); `src/contract/stablecoin/src/entrypoint.rs:1215-1240` (`process_mint_stable_instruction` reads `CDP_TOTAL_DEBT_KEY` and neither of those fields); `src/contract/stablecoin/proof/mint_stable.zk` (no `less_than_or_equal`); `open_position.zk:74`, `add_collateral.zk:80`, `remove_collateral.zk:80`, `repay_stable.zk:80` | found 2026-09-23 in `OBL-Z18`'s stablecoin slice as evidence inside `OBL-C83`, and filed separately while closing that row — because *"a mint does not violate pool collateralization"* is a proposition about pool solvency, not about binding a position operation to the position it acts on | M | **NEW 2026-09-24, filed from `OBL-C83`'s own evidence rather than folded into it, and the separation is this register's rule about one id naming one proposition.** *Three facts, each read on both sides.* (i) The struct states the property and promises the inputs for it: `MintStableParams.proof` carries the doc comment *"ZK proof: mint doesn't violate pool collateralization"* and the struct carries `total_debt` and `total_collateral` marked *"for ratio check"* (`model/mod.rs:516-523`). (ii) `process_mint_stable_instruction` reads **neither field** (`entrypoint.rs:1215-1240`): it reads `CDP_TOTAL_DEBT_KEY`, adds `params.mint_amount`, and writes the sum. `CDP_MIN_RATIO_KEY` — written at init from `params.min_collateralization_ratio` (`:192`) and rewritten only by `UpdateConfigV1` (`:828`) — is read by no mint path at all. (iii) The circuit does not carry the check either: `mint_stable.zk` has no `less_than_or_equal`, while each of its four siblings ends with one (`open_position.zk:74` "collateral >= 2 * debt", `add_collateral.zk:80`, `remove_collateral.zk:80`, `repay_stable.zk:80`). *What an unconstrained mint can write, and both consumers were read in `OBL-C82`'s pass.* `CDP_TOTAL_DEBT_KEY` is the denominator of the redemption exchange rate (`collateral_return = redeem_amount * total_collateral / total_debt`, `:1701`) and the input to the liquidation gate (`collateral_ratio = total_collateral * 10000 / total_debt`, compared at `:1279`). Mint is not authority-gated: any account that can fund one `promissory_note::transfer_v1` child of `mint_amount` can raise the recorded total debt by `mint_amount`, and raising it is what *opens* the liquidation gate — the check refuses when the ratio is at or above the threshold, so inflating the denominator is one way to make the pool liquidatable. *Why M and not higher, on the bar the register applies to the rows beside it.* The value path is bounded one layer down and that is a read, not a deferral: `transfer_v1`'s inputs each carry a `Revoke_V2` proof (`promissory_note/proof/revoke.zk:47-72`) and `verify_value_conservation` (`promissory_note/src/entrypoint/mod.rs:516-557`) forces the output token set to equal the input set with equal Pedersen sums, so a mint cannot create value; and the liquidation payout is funded by the transaction's own notes, not the pool, because every liquidate arm's ledger writes are totals and no stablecoin client builds the child transfer. What remains is the same shape `OBL-C102` states for `drain_protection`: the contract's bookkeeping moves and its money does not, so the harm is that the *recorded* pool state — the one the redemption rate and the liquidation gate are computed from — is writable by anyone without the constraint its own documentation claims. *Repair, and the half that is a design decision.* The host half is small: read `CDP_MIN_RATIO_KEY` and both totals in `process_mint_stable_instruction` and refuse the mint whose resulting ratio falls below the minimum, then either check `params.total_debt`/`params.total_collateral` against those reads (making them live) or delete them as a second source of truth. The circuit half is not the siblings' shape and cannot be: their `less_than_or_equal` compares a *position's* own figures, while a pool ratio needs the pool's totals, and a witness is prover-chosen — the lesson `OBL-C82` records when it refuses a checked rate with a caller-chosen period. So which of the two the property should mean — the position's collateralization or the pool's — is a decision to take deliberately rather than a patch to apply. *Why it is not repaired here*: the control needs a fixture this spec cannot build. Crossing `CDP_MIN_COLLATERALIZATION_RATIO` (15000) requires `total_debt > total_collateral * 10000 / 15000`, and with the fixture's 10000 collateral that is a mint above 5666; the largest note the fixture issues is 5500 and it is already spoken for, so a negative endpoint would be refused for the wrong reason — the exact failure mode `OBL-C83`'s first run demonstrated and corrected. Recorded rather than attempted so the next reader starts from the fixture constraint instead of rediscovering it. **CLOSED 2026-09-24: the check is in, and the fixture constraint this cell named turned out to be the wrong obstacle.** *What the cell said was blocking it*: crossing 15000 with the fixture's 10000 collateral needs a mint above 5666, and the largest note was 5500. **The arithmetic was right and the conclusion was not** — a second mint does not have to be funded by a new note of the amount that crosses the line, because each mint leaves a *new position* behind: 1000 (note n[3]) takes the pool to 1000 debt, 5500 (the unused n[5]) takes it to 6500, and a third 500 (n[4], which the repay endpoint below it also needs and still finds) would take it to 7000 — 14285 bp against the 15000 minimum. So the fixture was one chained mint away from the control the cell thought it could not build; the obstacle was not the note size but the assumption that one mint was all the arm could be driven through. *The repair.* `process_mint_stable_instruction` now reads `CDP_MIN_RATIO_KEY`, `CDP_TOTAL_COLLATERAL_KEY` and `CDP_TOTAL_DEBT_KEY`, computes the pool's post-mint ratio, and refuses a mint that would take it below the minimum, naming both figures. It is host-side and that is not a preference: a *pool* ratio is not a position's figures, and `open_position.zk`'s sibling check compares the position's own witnesses precisely because those are the only operands a circuit has — every one of them prover-chosen, so a ratio over them is satisfiable by choosing them, which is the lesson `OBL-C82` records about a checked rate beside a caller-chosen period. This reads the contract's own stored totals, which the caller cannot choose. *And the second source of truth is gone rather than left beside the check.* `MintStableParams` carried `total_debt` and `total_collateral`, marked *"for ratio check"* — the fields this cell's evidence cites at `:516`/`:520`, now deleted, so those line numbers describe the pre-repair file — and the log line above the debt read printed `params.total_debt`, i.e. the caller's claim about the pool under a label that read like the pool's own state. The fields are deleted (layout 66→50 bytes before the proof) and the log now prints `pool_debt`, `pool_collateral` and `min_ratio` from the reads, once. The struct's proof comment — *"ZK proof: mint doesn't violate pool collateralization"* — is now true as written, and says so. *Controls, and the run that failed first is worth recording because it is `OBL-C83`'s lesson in a new disguise.* Three mints in a chain, of which the first two are accepted at measured ratios (1000 → pool 1000, then 5500 → pool 6500 **at 15384 bp, above the threshold** — so the fixture also states where the threshold sits) and the third refused: `[stablecoin::MintStable] Error: mint would leave the pool at 14285 bp against a 15000 bp minimum`, block rejected. **The first run refused that third mint for the wrong reason**: `pn_transfer_child` fixes every field of its output commitment but the value and the spend_hook, so a below-minimum mint of 1000 with this contract's hook reproduced the *positive* mint's output commitment, and the promissory note turned the child away — `[transfer_v1] Error: Duplicate commitment in output 0` — before the ratio check was ever reached. That is the same shape as `OBL-C83`'s misplaced negative endpoint: a refusal that holds for a reason the endpoint does not name. Fixed by spending the 500 note instead, after which the log carries no duplicate-commitment line at all (measured: zero occurrences). *The pre-fix half is measured too, not inferred.* With the check neutered the run reports **`TEST-FAIL [stablecoin::MintStableV1BelowPoolMinimum]: expected rejection but accept_block succeeded`** — the endpoint is accepted, the position consumed, the pool's debt written past its floor — and with it restored, `test_heavyweight_stablecoin` is **ok, 678.33s**. The artifact is pinned to the statement: `src/contract/stablecoin/.source_hash` is `94d4f2f6…` both before the neuter and after the restore, so the binary that passed is the binary on disk. *Two fixtures moved with the debt trajectory, which is the coupling `OBL-C104` names and each refusal was read verbatim rather than assumed*: `GovernanceReportV1` now reports `total_debt` 6000 instead of 500 — the first run's refusal was `[stablecoin::process_instruction] GovernanceReport: debt mismatch — reported=500 on_chain=6000` — and both accrue endpoints take `old_total_debt` 6000, the stranger's endpoint changing nothing but its secret, which is what makes it a control. *Residual, stated rather than implied*: the property this row holds is the **pool's** collateralization, which is what the struct's comment claimed. A position's own collateralization *on mint* — `new_debt` against `new_collateral` at the configured minimum, the shape the four sibling circuits enforce — is still unenforced, and it is a different proposition: the pool's total is the contract's own state, while a position's figures are the prover's. Whether `mint_stable.zk` should carry one is a design question this row does not take. And the position/pool ledger decoupling is untouched: a mint raises `CDP_TOTAL_DEBT_KEY` by `mint_amount` without reference to the position's own `new_debt`, so the two ledgers are not reconciled by this check or by anything else |
| OBL-C120 | **OPEN** | No node relays or accepts a block whose timestamp lies beyond a bounded interval ahead of local time — the relay policy the code says it has | `src/linear/src/validation.rs:197-198` (the claim); **nothing in `bin/` or `src/` implements it** | found 2026-09-24 while correcting `OBL-C110`'s specification | M | **NEW 2026-09-24: a claim of enforcement with no enforcement behind it — the `OBL-C111` species, found by grepping for the thing being cited, which is the check the doc comment's own assertion was never given.** The comment read "The future-timestamp check (Bitcoin Core's MAX_FUTURE) is a network policy, not a consensus rule. It is enforced at the P2P layer before relaying a block." **Measured: it is enforced nowhere.** Grepping production paths (`bin/`, `src/linear/`, `src/sdk/`, `crates/`) for `MAX_FUTURE`, `max_future`, `future_timestamp`, `FutureTimestamp` and the phrase `in the future` returns exactly **one** line — that comment — and `bin/dwowd/src/proto/linear_broadcast.rs` has **zero** `timestamp` matches, so the relay path never reads the field. The only rule on a timestamp anywhere is `check_block_timestamp`'s median check, which is a *lower* bound. **What an unbounded future timestamp costs, read from the retarget rather than asserted:** `compute_adjustment` (`src/linear/src/consensus.rs:483-543`) takes the last 10 timestamps, sums the 9 intervals between them, and **abandons the adjustment outright when any interval is negative** (`:499-505`, the `OBL-C37` guard, whose own comment says why a fabricated interval must not be manufactured). A block dated far ahead makes every window containing it abort, because the next block's interval into it is negative, so **one miner, with one block and no extra hashrate, freezes difficulty adjustment for up to 10 blocks** — and the median rule cannot see it, since a future timestamp is above the median by construction. The sharper direction needs a window rather than a block: a party holding the last 10 timestamps moves `avg_interval` and drives the target up to 10% per block (`:526-542`) toward `min_target` or `max_target`. **Why M and not H**: the free effect is a *freeze*, which mints nothing by itself, and the paying direction — easier mining — still requires the majority of a 10-block window, the same premise the finality rows already price. **What would close it**: a relay-layer bound (Bitcoin Core's two hours) as a **node-local policy, never a consensus rule** — the reason the row above gives, and the shape `OBL-C67`'s monerod confirmation depth already takes, since a predicate reading the local clock is not a function of block data (`type-system.md` §9). Until it exists the comment may not claim it; **that half is corrected today**, in the same change as `OBL-C110`'s |
| OBL-C121 | **FIXED** | A comment's statement about what the model and the deployment carry states the counts the gates measure | `src/sdk/src/capability.rs:236-239` | found 2026-09-24 as F10 of the wallet-Lean plan; measured here with `contrib/barb_alphabet_diff.sh` | L | **NEW 2026-09-24: a false statement about the code, in a comment that is *trying* to be honest — and the honest repair costs the re-roll batch, so it is recorded rather than spent.** The comment reads: the previous wording "Mirrors the Lean4 `inductive Barb` in Types.lean" "was false in both directions: the Lean model carries **22** barbs and `dwow_core::barb::BarbId` carries all **32**". **Measured, and both numbers are wrong**: `bash contrib/barb_alphabet_diff.sh` reports `counts: doc 33, lean 33, core 33, sdk 14, py 14`, with `core BarbId == §1.1` HOLDS and `Lean Barb == §1.1` HOLDS — so the two counts the comment asserts are **33** and **33**, and the sentence's framing ("false in both directions") describes a gap that no longer exists in either direction: what remains is `sdk ⊂ §1.1` (19 barbs in the alphabet and not in `sdk Barb`, listed by the gate), which the 14-vs-33 shape of the surrounding prose already says correctly — "the 14 rows 1-14" matches the measured 14. So the defect is exactly two numbers, and their direction is the *opposite* of the one the comment implies: the model and the deployment agree on the alphabet; it is the SDK's capability subset that is smaller, which the comment's own next sentence states. **Why L**: nothing behaves differently — a reader is misled about the model's coverage, and the row exists because the same species (*a claim about the code that the code contradicts*) has been H and C elsewhere in this register. **Why it is not fixed in this change, which is a decision and not an oversight**: `src/sdk/**` is in **every** contract's `SOURCE_MANIFEST` (`contract-source-hash-scope`), so editing the comment invalidates all 32 artefacts and re-rolls the genesis pin — and the standing rule is that every remaining pin-moving item batches into **one** second re-roll, not several. The precedent is in the tree already: `scripts/check-doc-index.sh` carries a declared exception (`:181`) for a citation in `src/sdk/src/blockchain.rs` of the withdrawn mining-tokenomics document — its filename is deliberately not repeated here, since naming it is what that exception exists to permit in the two files that may — whose recorded reason is that the file "is covered by EVERY contract's SOURCE_MANIFEST, so editing the comment invalidates all 32 artifacts and re-rolls the genesis pin — a cost a docs change should not impose, recorded instead". Same shape, same answer. **What closes it**: the two numbers, set to the measured 33 and 33, in whichever change spends the batch. A re-measurement is one command and its output is quoted above, so the row cannot go stale silently **THE DEFERRED BATCH, given one home on 2026-09-24 because it was accumulating in four rows and nowhere in particular**: *this* row's two numbers, `OBL-C62`'s decoder constant, `OBL-C16`'s fail-closed `ContractId::ZERO` guards and `OBL-Z17`'s possession plumbing are each an `src/sdk/**` or genesis-contract edit, so every one of them moves the genesis pin and invalidates all 32 contract artefacts. The decision of record is to pay that **once**, in a single batch, when something needs the pin to move for its own sake — never dripped, and never for a comment. This is the *second* re-roll budget; the consensus-format change (`5c6bf01a7c`) spent the first. **What that means for a reader of this row**: the barb counts in `capability.rs` are expected to be wrong today, and correcting them is a candidate for the batch rather than a defect to file — which is why the row is OPEN and not FAILS, and why "a wrong number sits in a comment" is not evidence that the count is unknown. Measured, one command: `bash contrib/barb_alphabet_diff.sh` **FIXED 2026-09-24, in the second re-roll batch, and the batch's *reason* turned out to apply to this row directly.** The comment now reads 33 and 33 and says the gate extracts five sets rather than four; the arithmetic and the reasoning that the *alphabet* agrees while `sdk ⊂ §1.1` is what is small are unchanged. Two things were learned on the way and are recorded here rather than in the commit alone. **First, why a comment is a pin-mover at all**: `src/sdk/src/**` file *line numbers* live in the contract wasm — a panic `Location` records `file:line` — so inserting five lines into `capability.rs` shifts whatever locations point into it, and the bytes move even though no behaviour changed. That is the mechanism behind the other session's `-Zlocation-detail=none` work, met here from the other side: it is why this row needed the batch rather than a drive-by, and it is checkable rather than assumed. **Second, the batch it rode:** `attestation` gained `CheckAttestationV1` (0x0d) so `labor_market/create_job`'s `attestation_id` has a callee that reverts on an unknown or inactive id, the contract was rebuilt clean, and the genesis pin was re-recorded from the ceremony's own value — `c8afdeb73f2c9f29839a0761b585beaccc7407b5bcf008bf9fbcaf992e166d9b`, replacing `c43ae8c36fa89f0807b5520d6ff7c7bb22187a243cac747ef4360d274c34a0c7`, with `genesis_pin_is_current` and `genesis_pin_rejects_a_wrong_hash` both green afterwards |
| OBL-C122 | **PARTLY** | A model's gate reports what the model measured — a green line means its checks ran and passed, not that the model printed something reassuring | `contrib/model/vm_state_model.py` (gate `scripts/run-all-tests.sh:347`), `contrib/model/dockernet_model.py` (gate `:335`) | the two runs below, each exactly as its gate invokes it | M | **NEW 2026-09-24, minted because the runner's own comment asks for it** — `scripts/run-all-tests.sh:320-326` says these two models "belong in the register as rows rather than being rewritten here", and no row existed. Measured by running each exactly as its gate does, and they came out **differently**, which is why the comment is corrected in the same change. **`vm_state_model.py` confirms the caveat, actively**: `python3 contrib/model/vm_state_model.py` exits **0** while printing `2/5 tests found crash paths` and four `FAIL` occurrences — because the file contains **no exit call at all**, so Python's default 0 is what the gate records. The `FAIL — …` lines are the model *demonstrating* a known crash path, which is its success; the gate's green therefore carries no information about those paths. It fails only by raising, and it has **17** `assert`s on its mitigations, so raising is possible rather than hypothetical. **`dockernet_model.py` does not confirm it, and the comment overstates it**: measured, `python3 contrib/model/dockernet_model.py` exits **0** with **0** `SKIP` lines, **0** `FAIL` lines and `ALL TESTS PASSED`. Its exit *is* tied to its failures — `:1410` `total_failed = failed + (0 if consensus_ok else 1) + (0 if pipeline_ok else 1)`, `:1416` `sys.exit(0 if total_failed == 0 else 1)` — and the eleven `SKIP (no wallet_model)` paths (`:855`–`:1144`) are **latent, not active**, in the gate's own invocation: `wallet_model` is importable when the file is run this way, so none is taken. What the comment described is a *conditional* failure mode needing that import to fail; stating it as the current behaviour is the same defect class as the counts this register spent the day correcting, in the direction of a caveat asserted rather than measured. **Why a row and not an edit to the models**: what `vm_state_model` *should* assert is a question about its purpose — it is a demonstration, not a test suite — and rewriting another author's model is not a records change. The record is what this row and the comment correction fix. **What would close it**: one decision per model — `vm_state_model` gains an exit tied to its findings, or leaves the gate list with the reason recorded; `dockernet_model`'s caveat is restated as the conditional it is (now done in the comment, so its half is a restatement rather than a repair) |
| OBL-C128 | **RESTATED** | A manifest's `proof_circuit` names a circuit that exists — the field the SDK loads a proving key by | `src/contract/labor_market/manifest.toml:76,88` and `src/contract/stablecoin/manifest.toml:74` | measured 2026-09-24 by joining every manifest's `[[functions]].proof_circuit` against the `circuit "…"` identities in that contract's `proof/*.zk`, and then reading both sides | M | **MINTED IN ERROR 2026-09-24, AND RESTATED THE SAME DAY: this is `OBL-C91`'s finding, not a new one, and this row's central claim was false.** It said "**no gate reads `proof_circuit` at all**" — and a check that reads it, and more, exists: `src/contract/test-harness/tests/manifest_proof_declarations.rs`, whose second test walks **every** contract shipping a manifest and a `proof/` directory and asserts four things — (A) a `requires_proof` function names a circuit its own manifest declares, **(B) a declared circuit is one a `.zk` builds**, (C) a built circuit is declared with zero exceptions, (D) a declared circuit is named by some function. Its `DECLARED_WITHOUT_CIRCUIT` table is (B)'s ratchet, and it lists **exactly the three sites this row filed** — `stablecoin`'s `RedeemStableV1` and `labor_market`'s `CreateJobWithCapabilityV2` and `CreateJobWithMilestonesAndCapabilityV2` — each with the row (`OBL-C91`) and a *better* reason than mine, plus count controls (25 contracts, 388 sites) so it cannot pass vacuously. **How the error happened, because it is a recorded class**: I grepped `scripts/` for a reader of `proof_circuit`, found none, and wrote the absence down — while the reader is a *test*, in the contract test-harness, reached by `make test` rather than by `run_gate`. That is `half-a-pair-error-pattern` in the same shape as the register's own earlier case ("my grep searched for `circuit_instance_derivation`, the *Python* file's name, while the wrapper's name is hyphenated, so the grep returned nothing and I wrote the absence down"). **A second cost, caught before it landed**: the test I wrote for it went into `src/sdk/src/manifest.rs`, and `SOURCE_FILES` is `find src -type f -name '*.rs'` **plus `find ../../sdk -type f -name '*.rs'`** — so the edit made `check-artifact-freshness.sh` report `FAIL: 32 stale artifact(s)` in the same run. It was reverted, the gate went back to `PASS: 32 artifacts fresh`, and the check it duplicated was already there. **What this row is now**: a record of a duplicate avoided rather than of a defect, kept because the duplicate was minted and the reasoning that produced it is repeatable. **The note as the row was first filed is kept below verbatim — the row was restated, not withdrawn, and this is the record of what it claimed:** **NEW 2026-09-24, found as a by-product of measuring `OBL-T7`'s `(r, s)` join and verified from both directions before filing.** Three manifests declare a circuit that does not exist, and **all three belong to live, dispatched functions**: `labor_market`'s `create_job_with_capability` (`code = 12` → `CreateJobWithCapabilityV2`) and `create_job_with_milestones_and_capability` (`code = 14` → `CreateJobWithMilestonesAndCapabilityV2`), dispatched at `entrypoint.rs:421` and `:454` with the enum's `0x0c`/`0x0e` agreeing with the manifest codes; and `stablecoin`'s `redeem_stable` (`code = 10` → `RedeemStableV1`), dispatched at `entrypoint.rs:491`, `:576` and `:791`. **Why that is functional rather than cosmetic**: `proof_circuit` is the field the SDK resolves a proving key by — `src/sdk/src/prover.rs:33-40` documents the join as "the function's `proof_circuit` names a `[[circuits]]` entry", and `contract_client.rs:413-425` performs it — so for these three the join resolves to nothing, which is what `OBL-C91`'s check (B) exists to catch. **Why nothing caught it in the shell gates, which is the true part**: `scripts/check-artifact-freshness.sh` checks namespace constants and reports **124 dead legacy constants**, but its two-class rule makes a `_V1`-suffixed constant a *report* rather than a blocker; and no **`run_gate` entry** reads `proof_circuit`. The claim that no *check* reads it was the false one |
| OBL-C77 | **CLOSED** | A metadata arm for a callable non-ZK function returns an **encoded** empty public-input vector, not a bare empty `Vec` | `execution.rs:423`; `native_token/src/entrypoint/mod.rs:922` (`plaintext_call_get_metadata`, the reference); contract-standards.md §3 | M | **The mechanism, on both sides, before any site: a bare empty buffer is not "no public inputs" to the host — it is the documented rejection signal.** `execution.rs` decodes the first encoded vector out of `metadata()` and, on an empty buffer, sets `fail_stage = "metadata-decode-zkp"` and fails the call, *before exec runs*. So an arm whose success path returns `vec![]` makes its instruction impossible to call, and the reason never appears in the contract's own log. The reference for the correct shape is `native_token`'s `plaintext_call_get_metadata`, which returns `zk_public_inputs.encode()` (an encoded empty vector) on success and the empty buffer only for its two rejection cases. **Measured, not inherited from the plan's line numbers** — those were stale, and the class is 12 arms across 11 contracts. Two of them were live defects, and they were in one contract: `relayer_endowment`'s `get_metadata` had explicit arms for three functions and `_ => vec![]` for **eight** more — WithdrawDeployment, SettleFees, UpdateConfig, ForceSettle, DeactivateEndowment and the three `relayer`-gated ones — with `// No ZK circuits for WithdrawDeployment, SettleFees, UpdateConfig` sitting directly above it. No circuits is exactly why they must answer with the *encoded empty* vector, and instead every one of the eight was rejected at the metadata stage: eight callable instructions that could never succeed, none of them ever exercised. They now each answer through a ported copy of the reference helper, with their params decoded first so a malformed call still gets the rejection signal, and the three gated arms carry the same `#[cfg(feature = "relayer")]` as their exec arms so the two dispatches stay in step. **The other ten are declared, not repaired, and the declaration is the review**: nine are `Initialize`/`InitializeV1` — the deploy-time instruction, whose exec arm either says so in words ("has no instruction data" / "has no update data") or returns `Err(InvalidFunction)`, and whose state is created by `init_contract` rather than by a call — and one is `bearer_bond`'s `VerifyCoverageV1`, whose exec function is a documented stub carrying the FIXME for the sentinel-key lookup it needs. For those ten the empty answer **is** the intended refusal; what was missing is that a reader could not tell that from an encode that had been forgotten. **A gate, because a shape check is the honest form of this rule**: `scripts/check-metadata-arms.sh` finds every `get_metadata` arm whose value is a literal empty vector, requires each to be declared in `script/metadata_plaintext_exceptions.txt` with the reason, and reports a stale entry — negative-controlled both ways (one entry dropped → exit 1 naming exactly that site; a fake entry → reported stale, run still passes).
