(3) `subscribe_v1` reads the plan back by `params.plan_id` and checks it (`:389`), so the fixture would need a real plan *and* a Merkle proof against it. *The spec header is the misleading half*: it says "5/5 endpoints active (2 empty_witnesses, 3 real ZK including 25-param subscribe)" — the placeholders are declared honestly, which is `OBL-C88`'s standard, but "active" is not what a run shows, and the run is the standard this row applies. *Consequence, in this row's terms*: this contract has no heavyweight coverage, so nothing here is evidence about it — the same shape as `OBL-C88` before its fixture was rebuilt, in a contract whose fixture is a larger job (a plan first, then PN children on subscribe and renew, with the notes and paths `drain_protection`'s spec now shows how to build). **PARTLY 2026-09-24: the fixture now registers what the arms need, and the run has moved two endpoints forward — to a proof failure that is not the fixture's.** *What was built.* A `setup` that issues the payment token and two notes against the genesis promissory_note contract; a **`DaoControlV1` endpoint first**, because `SubscribeV1` refuses without a plan and the only writer of the plans tree is that action — a **plaintext** function (no circuit, no metadata, no proof), and an endpoint rather than setup because the plan must be submitted to the contract under test, whose id the runner resolves at deploy time and the spec never learns; the id as `Subscription::derive_id(...)` (`OBL-C75`); the payment child with the blind the contract derives (`poseidon_hash([plan.price, commitment])`) and the Pedersen value commitment the circuit constrains; and the capability as `Subscription::access_capability(...)` over the **record's** fields — which the fixture learns through the subscribe endpoint's `verify_state` hook, because the record's `lock_until_block` is `current_block + plan.duration_blocks`, a block only the chain knows. *Measured, in the run's own logs*: `[subscription::dao_control_apply_v1] Plan stored: 1`, then `[subscription::subscribe_v1] Subscription created` and `[subscription::subscribe_apply_v1] Subscription stored` — so the plan lookup, the child, the `validate_child_value_commit` check and the id derivation all pass. **The block then fails at the L2 proof verify with `invalid proof: call[1] namespace 'SubscribeV2'`** — the subscribe's own proof, not its exec, and `call[1]` is the parent (the child is `call[0]`). *That is recorded as unexplained rather than attributed*: the instance count and order agree on all three sides (the metadata-alignment gate reports `subscription/subscribe — 3 constrain_instance, 3 metadata pushes, 3 client public inputs`), the client's witness list matches the circuit's declaration in order, and the values the fixture passes are the ones the arm publishes — but a proof that fails anyway is what `client_proof_self_verification.rs` exists to localise, and that test does not exist for this circuit yet. Filed as `OBL-C107`, which is the next step rather than a guess here. *And two of the six endpoints cannot pass for a reason this row does not own*: `CancelV1` and `RenewV1` build their proofs from `empty_witnesses` — the harness has no client for either circuit — so they are declared as placeholders in the spec's header and filed as `OBL-C106`. **Measured again 2026-09-24, after `OBL-C107` landed, and the run has moved three more blocks.** `SubscribeV1` is **accepted** (`[subscription::subscribe_apply_v1] Subscription stored`, block committed); `VerifyAccessV1` is **accepted and grants access** (`[subscription::verify_access_v1] Access granted`) — `OBL-C84`'s host comparison passing end to end for the first time, with the capability the record derives; and the negative half (`verify_access_wrong_plan`) is **rejected by the host**, its exec running twice and no L2 failure, which is the control doing what it exists for. Five functions, four contracts, none of them genesis, in three shapes and one that is not a defect at all. **(a) The field names a circuit the manifest does not declare — one character out.** `baccarat`'s `commit_bet` and `settle_bet` name `CommitBetV2` and `SettleBetV2`; the built circuits are `CommitBet_V2` and `SettleBet_V2` (`proof/commit_bet.zk:8`), the dispatch pushes exactly those strings (`BACCARAT_CONTRACT_ZKAS_COMMIT_NS_V2`, `entrypoint.rs:115,150`), and the manifest's own `[[circuits]]` section lists them correctly — so the *function field* is the wrong side, `find` returns `None`, and both instructions have no generic path. `OBL-C90`'s class test would catch this today if `baccarat` were in its manifest list. **(b) The declaration is of a circuit that was never built.** `stablecoin`'s `redeem_stable` names `RedeemStableV1` and `[[circuits]]` declares it, and the entrypoint pushes that namespace (`entrypoint.rs:475`) — while no `proof/*.zk` in the contract declares any `RedeemStable*`: the ten that exist are the other ten functions'. `labor_market`'s `create_job_with_capability` and `create_job_with_milestones_and_capability` are the same shape with the opposite intent, and they are the clearest case in the set: their metadata arms say *"No circuit exists yet — deferred to v1.1"* and return an encoded empty vector (`entrypoint.rs:421-430,454-463`) — plaintext calls — while the manifest declares both proof-bearing and the two `[[circuits]]` entries they name (`manifest.toml:141-146`) have no `.zk` behind them. **(c) Not a defect, and stated so it is not "repaired".** `native_token`'s `transfer` and `spend` declare `requires_proof = true` and name no circuit, carrying a comment that says why: each spends a `Burn_V2` and mints a `Mint_V2` in one instruction, and `ManifestFunction` carries a single `proof_circuit`. The type cannot express it. Its manifest is the FYI document its header says it is and deploys as `&[]`, so nothing reads it — but the row above's gate excludes it for this reason, and that exclusion is a fact about the schema rather than a dispensation. *Why no existing gate catches (a) or (b).* `circuit artifact freshness`'s check 2 compares Rust **constants** against the `circuit "…"` declarations in `proof/*.zk`, and reads its own severity off the constant's *name* — a dead `*_V2*` constant is a bug, a dead legacy constant is hygiene. `RedeemStableV1` is a **live** lookup key wearing the legacy name, so it is counted into "124 dead legacy constant(s)", where hygiene is what a reader expects to find. The manifest is a fourth artefact that no gate compares against the other three: the constants agree with the `.zk` files, and the manifest agrees with neither. *Why this is recorded rather than repaired, and the reason is not the difficulty.* None is a genesis contract, so each moves only its own `.source_hash` — but `labor_market` and `stablecoin` need a decision first: does the function get the circuit, or does the manifest stop claiming one? That decision is the work, and repairing the declaration without making it would bury a design question in a one-line edit. The `baccarat` half alone is one character in each of two lines. Severity M on `OBL-C90`'s reasoning: each is an operation whose generic path cannot be built, with the sharp end for `baccarat` and `stablecoin` being a wallet that cannot construct the call at all **APPENDED 2026-09-24 (later): this row's check was RED, on its count control, while the checks themselves were passing — and the distinction is the point.** Ran `cargo test -p dwow-contract-test-harness --test manifest_proof_declarations`: the first two tests pass, and `every_contract_manifest_agrees_with_its_own_circuits` panicked at `:442` on `assert_eq!(contracts.len(), 25)` with **`left: 31`** — its own `println!` reporting **31 manifests and 673 sites** against the asserted **25 and 388**. So `make test` was red, and **not because of a defect**: the panic is *after* the loop, so (A)–(D) had already run to completion over all 673 sites and passed. The counts were measurements of a 25-contract tree, and six more contracts now ship both a manifest and a `proof/` directory. **The numbers are re-measured and the control is kept** — a count that fails loudly on drift is the opposite of the hardcoded-coverage failure `OBL-C79` records, and what it must not become is a number nobody re-measures; the contract list is printed above it so a reader can tell "the tree moved" from "the parser stopped seeing things". Green after: 3 passed, 0 failed. **And this is the check that caught `OBL-C128`'s duplicate**: this row's `DECLARED_WITHOUT_CIRCUIT` table already named the three sites — `stablecoin`'s `RedeemStableV1` and `labor_market`'s two `CreateJob*WithCapabilityV2` — with better reasons than the duplicate carried, so the finding was recorded here all along and the new row was a re-discovery | M |
| OBL-C92 | **OPEN** | A redemption releases the collateral it claims, and the pool's ledger is written down only against debt that existed | `stablecoin/src/entrypoint.rs:1657-1658`, `:1694-1707`, `:1711-1727`; `stablecoin/src/model/mod.rs:985-988`; `promissory_note/proof/redeem.zk:50,53-55`; `promissory_note/proof/revoke.zk:70-72`; `promissory_note/src/entrypoint/mod.rs:1052-1057`, `:1070-1088` | **NEW 2026-09-23, found by the payout read `OBL-C82` held its severity open for.** That row asked *who funds the child transfer that pays the liquidator*; this is the third arm of the same contract, and the one where the ledger moves with no transfer asked for at all. *Both sides read, in one pass.* **The parent** (`process_redeem_stable_instruction`): it decodes the child, requires it to be `promissory_note::redeem_v1` (0x01) targeting PN, and then reads the child's output **only** for `spend_hook` (`:1660-1669`) — the value commits `validate_child_redeem_v1` returns are bound to `_receipt_value_commit`/`_receipt_token_commit` and discarded (`:1657-1658`). The payout it credits is `collateral_return = params.redeem_amount * total_collateral / total_debt` (`:1699-1704`), computed from the config DB and a **caller-chosen parameter**, bound only by `params.redeem_amount <= total_debt` (`:1694-1697`). The struct's own `total_debt` / `total_collateral`, documented *"Current total debt/collateral before redemption"* (`model/mod.rs:985-988`), are compared to the stored values **nowhere** — `params.total_debt` appears only in the log at `:1629` and inside the replay guard's hash (`:1714`), and `params.total_collateral` only in the log. **The child** (`promissory_note::redeem_v1`): merkle root exists, nullifier unspent, receipt commitment unique (`:1070-1088`) — and conservation is *deliberately absent* (`:1052-1057`: *"Redemption IS value destruction ... Value conservation is deliberately NOT enforced here"*). What the redemption actually pays is the receipt coin, and `redeem.zk` pins it to zero on both sides: `constrain_instance(value)` (`:50`) and `constrain_equal_base(value, ZERO)` (`:53-55`). *So the proposition is unenforced on both sides, and the payout is nothing.* The collateral release the property names is implemented **neither** in the child (a zero-value receipt) **nor** in the parent (which never binds `collateral_return` to anything the child moved). What a call does instead is write the pool's ledger down by an amount its caller chooses, up to `total_debt`, against a note of *any* value: the redeem input must be a real note — its `Revoke_V2` proof constrains merkle inclusion (`revoke.zk:70-72`) — but nothing binds that note's value to `redeem_amount`, and the "unique nullifier" `poseidon_hash([redeem_amount, asset_id, params.total_debt])` (`:1711-1715`) is built entirely of caller-chosen values, so it forbids an identical repeat and nothing else. *Why H and not C.* No value is minted, destroyed or stolen: the payer receives a receipt that is zero by circuit, and the collateral notes are untouched — they stay in the PN commitment set, spendable only by the holder of their `spend_secret`, and no arm of this contract can move one without it. What is destroyed is the **record** of what the pool owes, which is exactly the ledger `OBL-C82`'s `total_debt` and this `total_collateral` compose and which two consumers read (`:1701`, `:1279`): a stated safety property broken, not a value transfer. *The repair is two-sided, and the same shape as `OBL-C82`'s in the same order*: the child redeem must carry the collateral output the parent pays; the circuit must instance the input's `value_commit` so the parent can compare Pedersen sums against `collateral_return`; and the parent must then bind `collateral_return` to the burned value rather than to the ledger. *Not repaired here, for `OBL-C83`'s reason and one more*: `stablecoin/src/client/` holds **no redeem client** — its eight entries are `accrue_interest`, `governance_report`, `initialize`, `liquidate`, `mint_stable`, `mod`, `open_position`, `zkbins` — so there is no working flow to repair against, only a contract-side claim. Stablecoin is not genesis, so nothing here moves the pin | H |

| OBL-C93 | **CLOSED** | A node's shipped default configuration is one the node can actually load | `bin/dwowd/dwowd_config.toml` (`include_str!` into the binary, `main.rs:46`); `src/util/cli.rs:151-170` (write-then-reparse on first run); `bin/dwowd/src/main.rs:119` (`create_genesis: bool`, required) | no gate runner examines the template; measured by hand 2026-09-23 with `HOME`/`XDG_CONFIG_HOME` redirected to a scratch directory | M | **NEW 2026-09-23, found while verifying `OBL-C80`'s fix, and it is the failure that came first.** `BlockchainNetwork.create_genesis` is a required field (`main.rs:119` — a plain `bool` in a `#[structopt(long)]` with no `default_value`), and the shipped template carried it in **no** network section: `grep -c create_genesis bin/dwowd/dwowd_config.toml` → 0, and `git log -S create_genesis -- bin/dwowd/dwowd_config.toml` returns no commit at all, so the field was never there rather than removed by one. *Measured both ways.* A fresh `HOME` runs `dwowd` once, which writes the template and exits 2 with *"Config file created in … Please review it and try again."* (the documented first-run step); the **second** run — the shipped default, exactly as the operator was told to make it — then failed `ParseFailed("Failed parsing requested network configuration")` with *"missing field `create_genesis` at line 42 column 1"*, before any merge-mining or finality check could be reached. That is why `OBL-C80`'s consequence (i) was right about the conclusion and wrong about the cause: the node does not start, but not because of the `mm_rpc` gate. **Fixed here** by declaring `create_genesis = false` in the two *selectable* networks, `darkwow-devnet` and `darkwow-testnet` — the only two `main.rs:155-163` accepts, so the other three sections are unreachable and were left alone rather than given a field nothing parses. `--role genesis` / `CREATE_GENESIS=true` still overrides the value (`main.rs:276-280`), which is what leaves exactly one node per network able to run the ceremony. *Control, run on the shipped path rather than on a hand-built file*: with a binary rebuilt from this tree and `HOME`+`XDG_CONFIG_HOME` pointed at an empty directory, run 1 writes the template (`create_genesis` present, `mm_rpc` commented out) and run 2 reaches `Initializing a DarkWow daemon...`, failing only on `NODE_NAME not set` — the documented operational input, not a parse. Appending an `mm_rpc` section to that same written file makes it refuse with `merge mining requires monerod_url`, so the section is really parsed and the `OBL-C80` gate really bites |

| OBL-C94 | **CLOSED** | A log line names the network the node actually selected | `bin/dwowd/src/lib.rs:802` (`Dwowd::init_linear`) | measured 2026-09-23; `grep -rn "Initializing a DarkWow daemon" contrib/ scripts/ bin/` returns the source line and no consumer | M | **NEW 2026-09-23, found while controlling `OBL-C93`: a claim the log made that was not true.** `init_linear` logged `Initializing a DarkWow daemon for darkwow-devnet...` as a **literal**, so a node selected as `darkwow-testnet` announced itself as devnet. Measured rather than inferred, and with the CLI in the loop: two configs differing only in their top-level `network` key (`darkwow-testnet` and `darkwow-devnet`) both logged `darkwow-devnet`, and so did a run with `--network darkwow-testnet` passed explicitly — three runs, one answer, none of them the selected network. There was no way for it to be right in general: `init_linear` receives `network: Network`, and `parse_blockchain_config` maps *both* selectable networks to `Network::Testnet` (`main.rs:357-359`), so the specific network is not a value that function has. **Fixed** by dropping the clause rather than substituting a different literal — the message is now `Initializing a DarkWow daemon...`, which is also the exact form `doc/src/testnet/node.md:203` already quotes, so a citation that was approximately right becomes exact. Severity M and no higher: it mints nothing and gates nothing, and no script, test or pipeline phase greps the string, so nothing was mis-branched on it. What it cost is a debugger's attention — a node being told it is on the wrong network, in the one artefact people read when they do not trust the network — which is the whole reason it is recorded instead of silently deleted |

| OBL-C95 | **OPEN** | A container that enables merge mining supplies the verifier, or does not start | `contrib/docker/testnet-node/entrypoint.sh:152-158` (the `mm_rpc` write), `:354` (merge mode's config), `:357` (the node start) | invoked by no gate runner: `test_entrypoint.sh` tests this entrypoint and no target in `scripts/run-all-tests.sh` or the root `Makefile` calls it | M | **NEW 2026-09-23, found while repairing `OBL-C80`'s second consequence, in a script that row did not name.** In `MODE=merge`, `generate_dwowd_config "$CONFIGFILE" "true" "$MM_RPC_PORT"` appends `[network_config."${NETWORK}".mm_rpc]` (`:152-158`), and **nothing writes `monerod_url`** — `grep -n "finality\|monerod_url" contrib/docker/testnet-node/entrypoint.sh` returns only the monerod *process* management, no config write — and the node is then started as `/app/dwowd &` with no `--monerod-rpc-url` (`:357`). Against the gate `OBL-C80` landed, that is exactly the combination the gate exists to make unrepresentable, so this container's merge mode cannot start a node. **Not repaired here, and the reason is scope rather than difficulty**: the repair is the same one-flag shape as `test_merge_mining_p2pool.sh`'s — write `monerod_url` into the finality section, or pass the flag at `:357` — but this is a different script from the one the operator authorised for that edit, it runs in no gate, and *whether `MODE=merge` is still a supported path at all* is a question this row is better placed to ask than to answer with an edit. What is measured here is the code as read and the gate as run: the config this entrypoint generates cannot start a node under the current rule. Note also that its own test, `test_entrypoint.sh:311-322`, asserts `mm_rpc` **presence and absence by mode** and never starts the node — so it passes over this, which is the `OBL-C76` class again: a test that does not run in the configuration used to claim verification |

| OBL-C96 | **OPEN** | A milestone's payment is the amount the job records for that milestone, and a completed job's releases reconcile with the job's total | `labor_market/src/entrypoint.rs:1507-1522`, `:1514`; `labor_market/src/model/mod.rs:56-67` (`Milestone.payment_amount`), `:200` (`Job.released_payment`) | found by the `OBL-Z16` triage of `milestone_payment.zk`, in the same read that declared the circuit's instance safe; `labor_market`'s `confirm_milestone` arm read on both sides | M | **NEW 2026-09-23, and it is what is left after the circuit's half was found sound.** *Both sides, one pass.* The circuit's exposed `milestone_payment_amount` is forced to equal `params.payment_release` — the metadata arm publishes it (`entrypoint.rs:389`) and the runtime hands that vector to `verify_zkp` as the proof's public inputs (`src/linear/src/zk_verifier.rs:234-238`) — and `confirm_milestone_v1` binds the same params value to the child `promissory_note::transfer_v1` (`:1467-1472`). So the **transfer** is honest about what it pays. What is not compared is the **record**. The amount the job declares for the milestone is stored per-milestone at creation (`Milestone.payment_amount`, `model/mod.rs:60`) and read **nowhere**: every `milestones[...]` access in the host is `.completed` (`:1405`, `:1507` read, `:1512-1513` write), and every `payment_amount` the host consults is the *job-level* one (`job.payment_amount` at `:892`, `:1081`, `:1169`; `params.payment_amount` at creation). So `confirm_milestone_v1` marks the milestone complete and adds a caller-chosen amount to `released_payment` (`:1514`) with neither half of the reconciliation performed: the amount paid need not be the milestone's recorded amount, and when the last milestone completes the job is set `Confirmed` (`:1516-1522`) with `released_payment` never compared against `job.payment_amount` — that field is written and never read as a condition anywhere in the crate. *Why this is a record finding and not a value one.* No value is minted, destroyed or stolen: the child transfer is bound to the amount and draws on notes the transaction already owns, so a payer can only under- or over-pay out of its own pocket. What breaks is a stated property — the job record is the worker's and any arbiter's evidence of what was owed and what was paid, and "milestone N completed" is asserted against a figure the contract never consults. Severity M on the register's bar, which asks for a path from an untrusted input to value and finds none here. *The repair is one comparison, and which comparison is a decision rather than a reading*: either `confirm_milestone_v1` requires `params.payment_release == job.milestones[index].payment_amount`, rejecting partial payments, or it requires the accumulated `released_payment` to reach `job.payment_amount` before the job may be `Confirmed`. Both halves are half-written in the code, so the contract's intended model cannot be read off it, and this row records the finding rather than choosing one |

| OBL-C97 | **PARTLY** | Only the authority a protected fund registers may spend it, lock it or reconfigure it — the proof's point is compared against the fund's stored `spend_authority` | `src/contract/drain_protection/src/entrypoint.rs` (every process arm; `:487` stores the authority, `:788-793` rewrites it, and **no** arm reads it), `model/mod.rs:275` (`ProtectedFund::spend_authority`) | found 2026-09-24 while repairing `OBL-C78`'s metadata arms and `OBL-C88`'s harness — the first pass in which these arms had a client able to make a proof for an authority | H | **NEW 2026-09-24. The eight authority circuits prove knowledge of *a* secret; nothing binds that secret to the fund.** Read on all three sides. *The circuit* derives `authority_pub_x/y` from `authority_secret` and `authority_nullifier` from `poseidon_hash([1, fund_id, authority_secret])`, and instances all five values — so a proof says "I know some secret whose point and nullifier are these", which is exactly right and exactly not enough. *The metadata arm* publishes those five from the params — so verification ties the proof to the call, and to nothing else. *The host* never compares them to the fund: `spend_authority` is written by `init` (`:487`) and rewritten by `update_config` (`:788-793`) and **read by no validation anywhere** (`grep spend_authority` over the entrypoint returns those two writes and no read); `authority_nullifier` appears **only** in the eight metadata arms. The process arms validate that the fund exists, that it is not locked, and that the rate limit holds — never who is calling. *The consequence, walked rather than asserted, because this register does not rank a value path it has not walked.* No arm of this contract moves value: `apply_transfer_update` only *records* the transfer (`:1007-1008`) and the child it requires is a `promissory_note::transfer_v1`, whose inputs each carry their own `Revoke_V2` proof and are therefore the **caller's own notes** (the read `OBL-C82` records), and the contract has no spend hook (`grep spend_hook` over the crate returns nothing). So an unauthenticated caller gains no value; what it gains is the ability to **rewrite any fund's state**: lock it (a liveness denial of the fund's own protections), rewrite its `spend_authority` through `UpdateConfigV1` (inert today, and inert only because of this row), and write transfer records that can only *tighten* the rate limit, never loosen it. The stated safety property — only the authority may spend a protected fund — is therefore not enforced, which is `H` on this register's legend; it is not `C` because the value path above is bounded one layer down. *The fix is `OBL-C82`'s shape and was not taken here*: the host half compares the params' point against `fund.spend_authority` in each arm that holds a fund (`transfer`, `lock`, `unlock`, `update_config`, and `initialize` against the authority it registers), and the arms without one — `propose`, `vote`, `execute` — need the proposal-to-fund binding read first, which `OBL-C98` puts in the same place. The fixture is already written to match (`test-harness/src/harness/drain_protection.rs`: one authority secret, and `initialize` registers its point), so adding the check does not require rewriting it **PARTLY 2026-09-24: the five arms that hold a fund now compare, with a control that is a pair, and the residual is the arms that hold no fund.** *What landed.* `require_fund_authority` (`entrypoint.rs`, above `propose_process_instruction_v1`) compares the proof's `authority_pub_x/y` against `fund.spend_authority`'s point, refusing with `InvalidSpendAuthority` and naming the mismatch in a log line; it is called in `transfer`, `lock`, `unlock` and `update_config`, each immediately after the fund is decoded, and `initialize` gets the same comparison against the authority it is about to register (the fund does not exist yet, so both halves come from the call — the point the circuit derives and the point being registered, which is what keeps a creator from naming an authority it cannot use). The point is compared rather than the nullifier, and the reason is written at the function: the point is what `initialize` stores and `update_config` rewrites, while the nullifier is the circuit's own fund-to-secret binding. *The control, and it is two endpoints because one would not be a control.* `update_config_by_stranger` submits the same call built by the same client with a different secret — a **real** proof of the real circuit, every other precondition identical (the fund exists, it is unlocked, the call changes nothing else). Run: the log carries *"authority is not the fund's registered spend authority"* while the legitimate `update_config` in the same run is accepted and the run continues past both — so the refusal is this check rather than a proof failure, and a host that had merely started refusing everything would have failed the positive. The pair sits immediately after `initialize` in the spec, before the proposal flow, so both halves run before the run's later failure. *The residual, stated.* `propose`, `vote` and `execute` hold no fund — their arms look the fund up by a **proposal** or **multisig** id (`OBL-C98`), which is the same missing binding seen from the other side — and `exit`'s circuit instances only the tx pair, so its authorization cannot come from the proof at all: its witnesses for DAO membership are declared and its circuit block never reads them. Which authority each of those four should prove against is the decision `OBL-C98` already needs, and it is not taken here |

| OBL-C98 | **CLOSED** | A proposal can be executed: `ExecuteV1` looks up the fund it belongs to and finds it, and the multisig it requires is one a fund can have | `src/contract/drain_protection/src/entrypoint.rs:579-585`, `:490`; `model/mod.rs` (`ProtectedFund::multisig_group_id`) | found 2026-09-24 by the `OBL-C88` fixture, whose failure moved to this row when the proofs became real | M | **NEW 2026-09-24: the instruction cannot succeed, and it has two independent reasons.** *First*, `execute_process_instruction_v1` reads the **proposals** tree into `_proposals_db` (`:573`) and then looks the **funds** tree up by `params.proposal_id` (`:579`) — a proposal id used as a fund key, so the lookup succeeds only if a fund is stored under a proposal's id, which nothing writes. *Second*, when it does find a fund it requires `fund.multisig_group_id != pallas::Base::zero()` (`:583`, `Unauthorized` otherwise) — and `initialize` stores `multisig_group_id: pallas::Base::zero()` unconditionally (`:490`), with nothing in the tree ever writing that field. So even with the key right, the requirement is on a value the contract never sets. This is the `OBL-C77`/`OBL-C90` class — an instruction with no path that works — reached through a wrong key rather than an absent arm. *Measured, not read*: `test_heavyweight_drain_protection` now reaches `ExecuteV1` and fails there with the contract's own refusal, after `initialize`, `propose` and `vote` were accepted. *The relation to `OBL-C97`*: both live on whether an arm knows which fund it is acting for. `propose_process_instruction_v1` has the same shape one step milder — it looks the funds tree up by `params.multisig_group_id` (`:520`), a field whose name says multisig and whose key is the funds tree, so a caller must pass the fund's id in the multisig slot for a proposal to be recorded at all. That quirk is why the `OBL-C88` fixture passes `FUND_ID` there, and it is named here rather than repaired because which field should carry which key is the same decision `OBL-C97`'s fix needs **CLOSED 2026-09-24: the fund is what the three calls name, and `ExecuteV1` now succeeds — measured.** `ProposeParamsV1`, `ExecuteParamsV1` and `VoteParamsV1` each gained a `fund_id` (appended **last**, after the authority quintet, so every position the previous format had is unchanged and an old-format call is refused by the length check), and the three arms look the fund up by it: `propose` instead of by `multisig_group_id`, `execute` instead of by `proposal_id`. `OBL-C97`'s check follows on the fund each arm now holds — `propose`, `vote` (attached to a real fund; the *voter* is not the authority and the arm says so rather than pretending otherwise) and `execute` — which is what closed that row's residual for the proposal flow. *The measurement, which is this row's own criterion*: `test_heavyweight_drain_protection` passed `initialize`, both `update_config` endpoints (one accepted, one refused by name — `OBL-C97`'s control) and `propose`, `vote` **and `execute`** — it fails at height 7 in `ExitV1`, which is the *next* endpoint and a different reason (`Expected 1 child call (promissory_note::transfer_v1), got 0`: the exit fixture needs a payment child, as the subscription fixture did). Before this change the same test failed *inside* `ExecuteV1` at height 6 with the contract's refusal. *One correction to this row's own filing, made here rather than left.* It said the nonzero-`multisig_group_id` requirement stood "on a value the contract never sets". That is half wrong: `initialize` stores zero, and `update_config` **can** set it (`:836-837`) — so the requirement was satisfiable, and the fixture satisfies it by calling `update_config` first, which is part of what made the endpoints a flow. The only defect here was the key |

| OBL-C99 | **CLOSED** | A client builder's params are the params the contract decodes | `src/contract/drain_protection/src/client/mod.rs` (the three builders), `scripts/check-client-params-alignment.sh` (the gate), `script/client_params_alignment_exceptions.txt` | found 2026-09-24 while writing the `OBL-C88` fixture, which could not use the builders for these three | M | **CLOSED 2026-09-24: the three types are deleted, the builders return the model's, and the class is gated — with its width measured rather than assumed.** *The repair is this row's own first option*: `InitializeBuilder`, `ExecuteBuilder` and `TransferBuilder` now return `InitializeParamsV1`, `ExecuteParamsV1` and `TransferParamsV1` — the types the entrypoint decodes — so the divergence the row describes is **unrepresentable** rather than corrected, there being one encoder per call. Each gained what it was missing: `InitializeBuilder` an `instance_seed`, an `authority` and the quintet; `ExecuteBuilder` a `fund_id` and an `authority`; `TransferBuilder` a `fund_id` and an `authority` — the five authority values from one `AuthorityCallData`, which `create_authority_proof` also uses, so params and proof cannot disagree (`OBL-C78`). Rule (A) is **name-based and therefore shallow**: it catches the shape that occurred, not a second params type named something else, and its header says so rather than implying coverage. Its two live findings are declared in `script/client_params_alignment_exceptions.txt` naming `OBL-C103`, because those client files are another session's in-flight rewrite — an exception there is the record of a reading, not a dispensation, and its removal is exit 2. | OBL-C100 | **FIXED** | Disjointness of the per-call write sets a schedule is built from makes those calls' effects order-independent — the safety condition `type-system.md` §9.2 rests on | `src/linear/src/execution.rs` (`per_call_keys`: the before/after overlay snapshot at `:381-386` and the subtraction at `:547-551`); `src/linear/src/schedule.rs` (`ExecutionSchedule::build`) | found 2026-09-24 while mechanizing §9.2 in `proofs/lean/src/DarkFi/Semantics/Ledger.lean`, and proved there as `presence_diff_is_not_sufficient` | M | **NEW 2026-09-24. The write set the schedule is built from is a delta, so a call that rewrites a live key records as touching nothing.** `execution.rs` snapshots `before := cache.keys() ∪ removed` before a call and computes `call_keys := (cache.keys() ∪ removed) \\ before` after it — the keys the call newly *touches*, where touched means "has a record in `cache` or in `removed`". A key that is already present and is overwritten with a *different value* is touched before and touched after, so it lands in neither call's set: two calls overwriting the same live key record as **disjoint**, and they do not commute. *Mechanized rather than asserted*: `Semantics/Ledger.lean`'s `presence_diff_is_not_sufficient` refutes the tempting statement with an evaluated witness — at a store where every key holds `[9]`, one call writes `[1]` and the other `[2]` to the same key, both recorded sets are empty, and the two orders disagree at that key — and `diff_single_dom_self` is the other half, that the value-sensitive notion the module models does catch what the delta misses. *Impact today is nil, and that is not the point*: `ExecutionSchedule::build` is consumed only by an `info!` line (`:690-708`, "This does not change execution order — it logs what COULD be parallel when wasmer supports concurrent Runtime instances (Tier 6)"), so calls execute sequentially and nothing reads the deltas as a safety condition. The point is that when the Tier 6 path is built, the test as it stands admits a nondeterministic wave — and nothing else in the tree pins that. *The fix and its shape*: compute the write set from the value-sensitive overlay diff rather than the presence delta, which is what `Semantics/Ledger.lean`'s `Diff.dom` models and what `exec_perm` is proved against; `diff_dom_of_apply_ne` is the bridge that makes writing a different value a write. `type-system.md` §9.2 carries the caveat, and its citation of a "bisimulation witness" at `execution.rs:398-405` was corrected in the same pass to the real conflict checks at `:677` and `:741` **— advanced 2026-09-24, and the status moves `OPEN` → `FAILS` because the proposition as written is measured false.** The Lean layer now carries both directions of the comparison this cell described only in prose, and the reason it *was* prose is worth keeping: the gap was a **vocabulary** gap, not a modelling one. `Ledger.presenceDiff` states the delta over a `Store`, which cannot tell "a key the call removed" from "a key nobody mentioned"; the overlay delta can, because `Diff.val` has that third case (`some none`). So `rustPerCallKeys` is `per_call_keys` itself — the overlay's opinion after the call minus its opinion before — written in the vocabulary that can express it, with no new machinery. `rustPerCallKeys_subset_touched` proves the Rust's set is a **subset** of the touched set that `Diff.dom` names and `exec_perm` is proved against, which is the *unsafe* direction and is the statement that no safety argument follows from the deltas. `rustPerCallKeys_is_strict` proves the subset is strict, with exactly the case the `presenceDiff` note used to leave as prose — a key the overlay already had an opinion about whose opinion *changed*, written and then removed, where the recorded delta is empty and the effect is not. So three facts now stand machine-checked: the delta misses keys that change; the miss is not hypothetical; and where a write set *is* the touched set, `exec_perm` gives order-independence. **What the code must change is unchanged and still not done** — compute the write set from the value-sensitive overlay diff rather than the presence delta, as this cell's last paragraph says. **FIXED 2026-09-24, hours later: the code now computes exactly that set.** `src/linear/src/execution.rs`'s per-call snapshot is the overlay's *opinion* about every key it mentions — `overlay_opinions`: `Some(value)` for a key the overlay holds, `None` for one it marks removed — and the write set is the *change* of that opinion (`changed_opinions`) rather than the appearance of a key. That is `Ledger.lean`'s `Diff.dom` written over the overlay, with no new machinery, which is this cell's own "vocabulary gap, not a modelling one" arriving as code. The bulk of the change is a **concurrent session's**; this pass completed it — as it stood the consumer still read the old `before_keys`, so the file did not compile — and verified it, and the commit message says the same rather than claiming the work. **The direction is conservative, and that is measurable rather than argued**: the new set is a *superset* of the presence delta it replaces. A key newly in `cache` or `removed` had no prior opinion and is still recorded; a key already held whose *value* changed, or which was removed, had a prior opinion and is now recorded **too** — so nothing the old computation caught is lost, and a wave partition built on these sets is never *less* serialization. **Verified**: `RAYON_NUM_THREADS=10 cargo check -p dwow_chain` exits 0, and `cargo test -p dwow_chain --lib schedule` reports 5 passed, 0 failed. **What this row does not claim is the proof**: no Rust test drives a call that overwrites a live key and asserts its recorded set, so the mechanism is pinned by construction and by this cell's Lean rather than by a test — and the reason the impact was nil still holds, `ExecutionSchedule::build` having no behavioural consumer until the Tier 6 path is built. That test is this row's next unit, and `FIXED` here means the code computes the notion `exec_perm` is proved against, not that anything measures it doing so. **`FAILS` rather than `OPEN` is the honest word**: `OPEN` reads as "a defect still present", which is also true, but the proposition as written is *false* with a witness, and the register already keeps measured-false propositions under this status. The impact stays nil for this cell's own reason: the schedule is a diagnostic, calls execute sequentially, and nothing reads the deltas as a safety condition — recorded so the Tier 6 work inherits the finding rather than the test. **The `FAILS`-rather-than-`OPEN` argument above is superseded by the `FIXED` append**: it records why *that* word was chosen while the proposition was measured false, which it no longer is, and it is kept because the reasoning is the audit history rather than a live claim about this row. **APPENDED 2026-09-24: the residual this cell named is closed, and the `VERIFIED` sentence it carries was empty.** The residual read "no test drives a call that overwrites a live key and asserts its recorded set"; five tests in `execution.rs`'s first test module now do — the overwrite, a removal, a reinstatement, an untouched key, an unchanged removal marker, and *the assertion that keeps the set about the defect rather than the fix*: that the presence delta records nothing for the overwrite. That case is `Ledger.lean`'s `rustPerCallKeys_is_strict` in Rust. **The `VERIFIED` sentence above is corrected rather than deleted, because how it was wrong is the more useful record**: the commit that completed this fix claimed `cargo check -p dwow_chain` exit 0, and that command never compiled this module — `src/linear/src/lib.rs:39` gates it `#[cfg(feature = "pow")]`, and `pow` is not a default feature (`src/linear/Cargo.toml`'s `default = []`; only `bin/dwowd` enables it). So the check passed on a tree where `execution.rs` was not built, which is `OBL-T12`'s class — an instrument whose reach was smaller than its claim — arriving in a human verification step rather than in a gate. The commands that do compile it are `cargo check -p dwow_chain --features pow` and `cargo test -p dwow_chain --lib --features pow`; the latter reports **5 passed, 0 failed**. The tests are hidden by that same feature gate, so they are declared in `script/hidden_test_exceptions.txt` and `OBL-C76`'s gate refused them until they were — which is how this correction was found |

| OBL-C101 | **CLOSED** | A proposal executes only with the approval its group requires, and executing it does something | `src/contract/drain_protection/src/entrypoint.rs:592-594` (the comment that places threshold checking in `execute`), `:611-630` (`execute`), `:1031-1037` (`apply_execute_update`), `:1040-1045` (`apply_vote_update`) | found 2026-09-24 reading the propose→vote→execute chain for `OBL-C98`; **and the design that repairs it was derived in the same read** — the fund is the anchor: a proposal belongs to a fund, so `ProposeParamsV1`, `ExecuteParamsV1` and `VoteParamsV1` each want a `fund_id`, the three arms look the fund up by it (instead of by `multisig_group_id` and `proposal_id`), and `OBL-C97`'s authority check follows on the fund each one then holds | M | **NEW 2026-09-24: the governance is recorded and never exercised.** *The votes are written and never read.* `apply_vote_update` files each vote in the votes tree keyed by `poseidon([proposal_id, voter_pubkey])` (`:1042`), and the only read of that tree in the whole contract is the duplicate guard (`:598`) — the votes are never counted. `vote_process_instruction_v1` says so itself and says where the count lives: *"This function records the vote intent; threshold checking is in execute"* (`:594`). *`execute` does not do it.* Read whole: `execute_process_instruction_v1` reads the proposals tree into `_proposals_db` (`:613`, underscore and unused), looks a **fund** up by the proposal's id (`:619`, which is `OBL-C98`), requires that fund's `multisig_group_id` to be nonzero (`:623`), and returns an update carrying `{ proposal_id, action: proposal_id }` (`:627`) — no vote is read, no threshold is consulted, no group is named. *And executing writes nothing.* `apply_execute_update` is `Ok(())` with the doc comment *"execution is the proposal's effect, carried by its own child calls"* (`:1031`) — but the dispatch validates no child call at all (`:386-388`, where `TransferV1` and `ExitV1` beside it each require exactly one), so there is no effect for a child to carry. *Consequence, and why this is `M` rather than `H`*: nothing is stolen because nothing happens — the instruction is inert rather than wrongly authorized, which is the `OBL-C77` shape again. What is broken is the property the module states about itself: `ProtectedFund::multisig_group_id` exists so that large withdrawals need a group's approval, and no code path counts an approval. **CLOSED 2026-09-24: the approval is the child, the execution is recorded, and the record has a reader — which is what makes the governance load-bearing rather than a ceremony.** *Where the threshold lives: not here.* `multisig::FinalizeV1` refuses below its group's threshold — `if consumed.len() < group.threshold as usize { … InsufficientSignatures }` (`src/contract/multisig/src/entrypoint/mod.rs:377-383`) — counting each named approval against a signature record that must exist and must be for *this* group and *this* message (`:354-375`), and a child that fails fails its parent's transaction. So `execute` requires exactly one child, checks its selector (`0x03`), its contract against the configured multisig id (read from the info tree and fail-closed; the constant existed and no arm had ever used it), and then the two facts only this contract can see: the child's `group_id` is the fund's `multisig_group_id`, and its `message_hash` is the proposal. Re-counting the threshold here would be a second source of truth that could disagree with the first. *The message is the proposal id, not the proposal's message hash* — `proposal_id = poseidon_hash([fund.id, message_hash])` — which is what binds an approval to one fund: an approval to execute fund A's proposal cannot be replayed as fund B's approval of the same action. *Executing now does something in this contract*: `apply_execute_update` writes the proposal id into the proposals tree — the first writer that tree has ever had — and the exec phase reads it to refuse a second execution, which is reachable because a group may legitimately approve the same proposal again with fresh signatures (the first approval's nullifiers are spent, so the second finalize needs new ones). `ExecuteUpdateV1` lost the `action` field it carried beside the id, assigned `params.proposal_id` at its only construction site and read nowhere. *And the record has a reader*: a rate-limited transfer used to require only that *some* proposal id was supplied — any id, including one nobody proposed. It now requires the proposal to have been **executed** (`db_contains_key(proposals_db, …)`), so "this transfer was approved" and "this transfer names an approval" are one fact rather than two. *Measured, in the contract's own logs*: `[ExecuteV1::apply] Proposal recorded as executed` for the accepted execution; `[ExecuteV1] Error: the approval is a different group's, not the fund's`; `[ExecuteV1] Error: the approval names a different proposal`; and `test_heavyweight_drain_protection` **ok** (543s), the setup creating the governance group on chain — three members, threshold two, the multisig contract's own `derive_group_id` called rather than re-implemented — and the fund registering it. *Three controls, each a valid approval the fund must refuse, and each able to fail*: `execute_foreign_group` has a **second** group (one member, threshold one) genuinely approve the same proposal, so the child commits and only the fund-versus-child group comparison stops it; `execute_foreign_message` has the fund's **own** group approve a different message, so again only the message comparison stops it; and `transfer_unexecuted_proposal` is the same transfer call with the same proof naming a proposal the fund's own group **approved** and nobody executed — the pair that distinguishes approved from executed. Remove any of the three checks and its endpoint starts succeeding. *Residual, stated rather than implied*: the proposal's message hash stays opaque — this contract has no action enum, so an execution's effect is the approval being spent plus the record, not a described action carried out, and a model where an execution carries validated children (as `ExitV1` and `TransferV1` validate theirs) is a further decision rather than a silent gap in this row. |
| OBL-C102 | **OPEN** | The value a fund holds is state the contract can, and the payout it authorises is paid by the fund rather than by whoever calls | `src/contract/drain_protection/src/entrypoint.rs:501` (`total_funds: 0`), `:1130` (`check_rate_limit`), `:757-761` (the transfer's child check), `src/contract/drain_protection/src/lib.rs:59-78` (the function enum); `src/contract/promissory_note/src/validation.rs:63-69` | found 2026-09-24 while repairing `OBL-C88`'s children, whose honest fix exposed the value path | M | **NEW 2026-09-24. Four facts, each read, and they compose into one sentence: the contract's money does not move, its bookkeeping does.** (1) `total_funds` is assigned **exactly once and as a literal zero** — `total_funds: 0` in the fund `initialize` constructs (`entrypoint.rs:501`) — and no other site assigns it; the remaining occurrences are reads (`:674-683`, `:702`, `:1130`) and the model's encode/decode (`model/mod.rs:714`, `:761`). (2) **Nothing can raise it**: the function enum is nine variants, `0x00`-`0x08` (`lib.rs:59-78`), there is no deposit, and `InitializeParamsV1` carries no such field (`model/mod.rs:300-315`). (3) So `exit_value` is identically zero (`:683`) and `rate_threshold` is identically zero (`:1130`), which means `amount > 0` is always rate-limited and every non-zero transfer needs the multisig path — and the rate limiter is a stub besides: the transfers tree it is handed is `_transfers_db`, unused, its "total recent" is a literal `0`, and its own comment says so ("This is a simplified check - in production, iterate over transfer history"). (4) **The payout need not be the fund's**: `validate_child_value_commit` scans the child's *outputs* for one whose value commitment is `pedersen(expected, blind)` and never constrains the child's *inputs* (`promissory_note/src/validation.rs:63-69`), and the contract never mentions `spend_hook` — zero occurrences in `src/contract/drain_protection/` — which is the mechanism that would make a note spendable only through this contract. *Stated at the strength the evidence supports*: no funds can exist to steal, so this is not a live loss; it is that the two halves of "the fund paid X" are unlinked. The `OBL-C88` fixture demonstrates the shape and states it honestly: the exit's child spends a note the member holds and returns the remainder as change, and the contract accepts that as the fund's payout. *Repair direction, and it is a contract rework rather than a patch*: make the fund's holdings a real quantity (a field on `InitializeParamsV1` or a deposit variant) and tie the fund's notes to the contract with a `spend_hook`, so `exit` and `transfer` can require the child to spend a note whose hook is this contract. Until then these two functions are bookkeeping, and the row is **OPEN** rather than a note on `OBL-C88`'s repair. |
| OBL-C123 | **OPEN** | A gate's pass means the model it runs asserted the property the gate is named for | `contrib/model/vm_state_model.py`, `contrib/model/dockernet_model.py` (both wired as gates) | found 2026-09-24 while wiring the model gates (`b8e4dc8069`) | M | **NEW 2026-09-24: two of the eighteen `run_gate "Python: …"` lines report more than their models assert, and that difference is this register's own subject — a green line whose subject is not what its label says.** `vm_state_model.py` **demonstrates** known crash paths: it prints `N/5 tests found crash paths` and **no exit status depends on that count** (the file has no `exit` call at all), so a run that finds them exits 0 and reads as PASS — right for a survey, wrong for a gate, because nothing states which number would be a failure. `dockernet_model.py` counts its own `SKIP`/`FAIL` lines as passes under its test runner, so its tally can be green over work it skipped. Both were wired with the rest because both **can** fail — a broken assertion in either propagates, which was measured — and the peer who wired them put the caveat in the comment above their gate lines rather than rewriting someone else's models, which is the right call. **What is owed**: a decision per model about what it should assert (for `vm_state_model`, most plausibly that a *known* crash path stays reproducible rather than that some exist), and until that decision lands the two lines must not be read as verification. Severity `M` for the failure mode it is: a false PASS, hiding a model whose simulation stopped finding what it was written to find. |
| OBL-C124 | **OPEN** | A checker's summary counts the classes it can distinguish, and names them | `scripts/check-circuit-metadata-alignment.sh:628` (the summary line) | found 2026-09-24 while diagnosing `lottery/initialize` | L | **NEW 2026-09-24: gate 2's summary lumped two classes under one label.** It printed `5 count mismatch(es) over 5 circuit(s)`, and one of the five was `lottery/initialize — circuit has 5 constrain_instance but no metadata push carries LOTTERY_CONTRACT_ZKAS_INIT_NS_V2`: a **missing-namespace** failure, not a count mismatch — that arm pushed nothing at all, so there was no vector to compare counts against. The verdict was right in every case, and the *label* is what misleads: a reader who greps the summary for count mismatches cannot find this class, and class-aware triage — which is how this register's rows get worked — is told the class does not exist. The one live instance is gone, because `InitializeV2` was deleted in `185c56d8a2` on the reading that a circuit whose key input is exec-derived cannot be proved at all; so the label defect now has no instance and is still a defect, since the next missing-namespace site will be reported as a count mismatch again — with a correct count. |
| OBL-C125 | **OPEN** | A binary in this tree compiles, or a gate says it does not | `bin/bridge_test_helper/src/main.rs`; **no gate builds it** | found 2026-09-24 while verifying the bridge repair (`5c83f85cee`) | M | **NEW 2026-09-24: `bin/bridge_test_helper` did not compile at HEAD, and nothing noticed because nothing builds it.** Its `deposit` call passed **seven** arguments to an eight-argument `BridgeHarness::deposit` — the missing one was `merkle_proof` — from whichever commit last changed the harness's signature. It is fixed in the bridge repair's commit, passing the same dummy three-element proof `bridge_spec.rs` uses, because a test helper needs the *shape* rather than a real Merkle path. **What the row exists for**: the crate appears in no `run_gate`, so a break there is invisible until somebody happens to build it by hand — which is how this was found, by a check being run for an unrelated reason. The general form is `OBL-C76`'s ("a test that does not run in the claimed configuration is not verification") one step stronger: a *binary* built in no configuration is not a tool. Either a gate builds it, or the row records that it is a manual tool with no guarantee behind it. |
| OBL-C126 | **OPEN** | A normative sentence names a state that the section it cites defines | `doc/src/arch/wallet.md:678` (§6.2), `:559`, §6.5's lifecycle; `bin/dww/src/capability.rs`'s `CapStatus` | found 2026-09-24 while modelling §6.2 for `Capability/Selection.lean` | M | **NEW 2026-09-24: three vocabularies for one spend state, and the single normative sentence cites a name its own cited section does not define.** `wallet.md:678` — a `SHALL NOT` — reads "A capability in the `Reserved` state (§6.5) SHALL NOT be selected", while §6.5's lifecycle runs `NULL → Pending → Processing → Spent` and the code's authority is `enum CapStatus { Pending, Processing, Spent }` (`bin/dww/src/capability.rs:29-33`, where `NULL` is the implicit not-in-the-map state — the enum's own comment says so). A third vocabulary appears at `:559`: "per-capability spend-state (`Unspent`/`Reserved`/`Spent`)". So `Reserved` and `Unspent` are names the code does not have and §6.5 does not define. **The intent is consistent across all four readings** — nothing spoken-for may be selected — which is why this is a vocabulary defect and not a behavioural one: an implementer can guess what is meant, and a *checker* cannot, because the sentence's subject cannot be looked up where the sentence says to look. The model side took the code's names as authoritative (`Capability/Selection.lean`, beside `HIGH-17`), on this tree's rule that the deployment's names are the names — so the document is the thing to correct, and `grep -c Reserved` over this register returned **0** before this row, which is why nothing had caught it. |
